California Supreme Court Invents Constitutional Right to Affordable Bail

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The California Supreme Court has once again mistaken judicial invention for constitutional interpretation. In April, the court held that, except in capital cases, the California Constitution guarantees criminal defendants a right to pretrial release on bail—and requires trial judges to set bail at an amount that is “reasonably attainable” by the accused.[REF] That holding is as novel as it is alarming. It elevates a judge-made entitlement over the text, history, and structure of the state constitution; discounts the grave risks that some defendants pose to victims and the public; and disregards the repeated, unmistakable commands of the California electorate.

Californians have confronted this pattern before. Again and again, the state’s high court has purported to discover sweeping rights for criminals in the California Constitution that the document’s text does not fairly support—only to have the people restore constitutional order at the ballot box. Propositions 4, 8, 9, and 189 were not isolated political reactions. Each was a referendum passed in response to radical decisions that placed the interests of criminals above the rights of victims, the protection of the public, and the common-sense judgments of the citizenry. Each reflected the sovereign authority of the people to correct a court that had strayed from the constitution it was charged with interpreting.

The latest bail decision repeats that error in particularly stark form. During the past 44 years, California voters have amended their constitution not once but twice, both to make clear that trial judges may deny bail to a person charged with a felony and to establish a Victims’ Bill of Rights—including mandatory detention for violent felons who pose a threat to others.[REF] Those amendments cannot be reconciled with the court’s new affordable bail standard. The court’s contrary conclusion does not merely misread the charter; it diminishes the constitutional choices the people themselves have made.

This Legal Memorandum proceeds in three parts. Part I traces the California Supreme Court’s recurring practice of constitutional innovation and the electorate’s repeated use of the initiative and referendum process to reject it, with particular attention to Propositions 4, 8, 9, and 189. Part II examines the text, history, and purpose of California’s bail provisions and Victims’ Bill of Rights, showing how those provisions preserve judicial authority to deny bail when necessary to protect victims and public safety. Part III analyzes the court’s April decision, identifies its doctrinal and practical defects, and explains why its “reasonably attainable” standard cannot be squared with the constitution as amended by the people.

The court’s latest holding is contrary to the text of California’s constitution, contrary to its history, and contrary to the settled will of the people. It should not be permitted to stand. The people of California—or, where constitutionally sufficient, their elected representatives—should correct the court once again and reaffirm a basic principle of ordered liberty: The criminal justice system exists not only to protect the rights of the accused, but also to vindicate the rights of victims, punish criminals, and safeguard the public.

Part I: Judicial Innovation v. the Electorate

California’s original constitution of 1849 provided that “all persons shall be bailable, by sufficient sureties; unless for capital offences, when the proof is evident or the presumption great.”[REF] Another provision stated that “excessive bail shall not be required, nor excessive fines imposed, nor shall cruel or unusual punishments be inflicted, nor shall witnesses be unreasonably detained.”[REF] In 1879, those two provisions were consolidated into Article I, Section 6 of the Constitution of 1879. That remained the case for almost 100 years.

In 1973, the California Supreme Court held in In re Underwood that the right to pretrial release was guaranteed for all defendants.[REF] Displeased with the court’s ruling, voters passed Proposition 7 in 1974, relocated the bail provisions to Section 12, and gave trial judges the discretion to release defendants on their own recognizance.[REF]

In 1977, Governor Jerry Brown appointed Rose Bird as Chief Justice of the California Supreme Court. Bird was divisive and controversial in part because of her consistent votes against capital convictions[REF] and a dissent she wrote opposing the inclusion of Proposition 8—the Victim’s Bill of Rights—on the ballot in 1982.[REF] She was eventually removed from office in 1986 by the voters.

By 1982, having had enough of rogue judges and violent criminals, California voters passed two propositions to amend the constitution to shorten the leash on judges and bolster victims’ rights. The first was Proposition 4, a constitutional amendment that allowed courts to deny bail in felony cases under two additional circumstances. As amended, Section 12 stated:

A person shall be released on bail by sufficient sureties, except for: (a) Felony offenses involving acts of violence against another person when the facts are evident or the presumption great and the court finds based upon clear and convincing evidence that there is a substantial likelihood the person’s release would result in great bodily harm to others or; (b) Felony offenses when the facts are evident or the presumption great and the court finds based on clear and convincing evidence that the person has threatened another person with great bodily harm and that there is a substantial likelihood that the person would carry out the threat if released.[REF]

The voters passed Proposition 4 by an overwhelming vote of 82.5 percent to 15.1 percent to prevent both the legislature and judges from changing the requirements that governed bail without the people’s consent.[REF]

The second was Proposition 8, which amended the state constitution by including a victim’s bill of rights and restricting the rights of convicts.[REF] Adopted by a vote of 56 percent to 43 percent, Proposition 8 repealed Section 12 of Article I and added Section 28, which included the following prefatory language: “The People of the State of California find and declare that the enactment of comprehensive provisions and laws ensuring a bill of rights for victims of crime, including safeguards in the crime justice system to fully protect those rights, is a matter of grave statewide concern.”[REF] Section 28(e), entitled “Public Safety Bail,” stated in part:

A person may be released on bail by sufficient sureties, except for capital crimes when the facts are evident or the presumption great…. In setting, reducing or denying bail, the judge or magistrate shall take into consideration the protection of the public, the seriousness of the offense charged, the previous criminal record of the defendant, and the probability of his or her appearing at the trial or hearing of the case. Public safety shall be the primary consideration.[REF]

Taken together, Propositions 4 and 8 sent a loud and clear message that the citizens of California were fed up with judges releasing violent felons, that mandatory release on bail was a bridge too far, that victims mattered, and that public safety—not defendant’s “rights”—was the “primary consideration” that judges must take into account when setting bail.

In 1994, the voters passed Proposition 189, which, among other things, amended Article I, Section 12 by adding “felony sexual assault offenses on another person” to subsection (b).[REF] A year later, in In re York, a case dealing with whether a court or magistrate may condition release from custody on a defendant’s own recognizance, the California Supreme Court noted in a footnote that the bail provision language in Proposition 4 appeared to conflict with the language in Proposition 8 (“shall” in Proposition 4 vs. “may” in Proposition 8) and that, because Proposition 4 received more votes, its bail and own recognizance provisions were “deemed to prevail over those set forth in Proposition 8.”[REF] In other words, trial judges “shall” (i.e., must) release defendants on bail unless it is a capital case or one of the felony exceptions enumerated in subsections (b) or (c). Remarkably, the court did not cite any statute or state constitutional provision authorizing it to erase the will of the voters and nullify Proposition 8 because Proposition 4 received more votes.

In 2006, the California Supreme Court held in People v. Standish that, because the bail provisions of Propositions 4 and 8 were “directly at odds,” Section 28(e) of Proposition 8 “never took effect.”[REF] So much for the 2,826,081 Californians who voted for Proposition 8.

Angered by crime rates and a state Supreme Court that did not put victims first, California voters passed Proposition 9, the “Victim’s Bill of Rights Act of 2008,” called Marsy’s Law.[REF] Proposition 9 specified that the law, named for Marsy Nicholas, who was murdered by her ex-boyfriend in 1983, was “needed to remedy a justice system that fails to fully recognize and adequately enforce rights of victims of crime.”[REF] In the “Findings and Declarations” section, Marsy’s Law says that “[t]he People of the State of California find that the ‘broad reform’ of the criminal justice system intended to grant these basic rights mandated in the Victims’ Bill of Rights initiative measure passed by the electorate as Proposition 8 in 1982 has not occurred as envisioned by the people,” a not-so-subtle swipe at the California Supreme Court and its abandonment of Proposition 8.[REF]

Marsy’s Law amended Section 28 of Article I of the state’s constitution by enumerating victims’ rights “in order to protect the legitimate rights of victims of crime.”[REF] Key among those rights were (1) the right to “have the safety of the victim and the victim’s family considered in fixing the amount of bail and release conditions for the defendant” under Section 28(b)(3) and (2) the right to public safety bail: “A person may be released on bail by sufficient sureties, except for capital crime when the facts are evident…. [I]n setting, reducing or denying bail, the judge or magistrate shall take into consideration the protection of the public, the safety of the victim, the seriousness of the offense charged…. [P]ublic safety and the safety of the victim shall be the primary considerations” under Section 28(f)(3).[REF]

Notice how California voters again adopted language to reject the California Supreme Court, this time to rebut the court’s creative erasure of the bail provisions of Proposition 8. By using “may” instead of “shall,” the people made clear both that judges do indeed have the discretion to deny bail and that the safety of the victim must be the primary consideration.

Part II: Cash Bail and Victims’ Rights

Cash bail rests on a straightforward proposition: Pretrial liberty and accountability need not be opposing principles. By placing a financial stake behind a defendant’s promise to return to court, bail supplies a concrete incentive to appear while permitting release pending adjudication—not punishment before conviction. From the perspective of the California District Attorneys Association (CDAA) and other prosecutor associations across the country, traditional cash bail remains an important component of a pretrial system charged with protecting the public and securing defendants’ appearance. Bail schedules give that system a practical framework: Predetermined, offense-based amounts provide a consistent starting point, make release requirements more predictable, and can allow eligible defendants to obtain release without waiting for an initial court appearance.

Properly understood, however, a schedule is a starting point, not a substitute for judicial judgment. Neither money nor a printed chart guarantees public safety or attendance; individualized review must account for the defendant’s circumstances and ability to pay, available nonfinancial alternatives, and applicable constitutional protections. The sound argument for bail schedules is therefore not that they eliminate discretion, but that they discipline its exercise, providing an orderly framework within which courts can reconcile liberty, accountability, and public safety.

As a policy matter, the push to end cash bail is not new in our country. In 1961, progressives created the Manhattan Bail Project specifically to eliminate cash bail.[REF] That project eventually grew into the Vera Institute and dozens of other organizations like Vera that are obsessed with ending cash bail. They even came up with spooky-sounding terms like “poverty penalty,” suggesting that the only reason a person remained in jail pending trial was his or her inability to post bond, ignoring the facts of the crime alleged or history of failures to appear in court or long criminal records—the Achilles’ heels of the movement. In 2017, former career public defender Robin Steinberg founded the Bail Project, an organization the provides free bail assistance to those who cannot afford to post bail, in California.[REF]

In 2018, the California Senate passed S.B. 10, a law designed to eliminate cash bail in the state and replace it with mandatory risk assessments for detained suspects awaiting trial.[REF] After the governor signed the bill, a unique mix of stakeholders banded together to repeal the legislation by referendum during the November 2020 election. Supporters of the “no” vote to repeal the controversial legislation included, in addition to the California Republican Party, the American Civil Liberties Union (ACLU) of Southern California, California Black Chamber of Commerce, California NAACP, and Crime Victims United of California, among other groups. The “yes” vote effort was backed by the California Democratic Party and included financial support from Patty Quillin ($1 million, the wife of Netflix CEO Reed Hastings; Steve and Connie Ballmer of Microsoft ($6 million total); and Tom Steyer, founder of Farallon Capital hedge fund and the NextGen Climate Action Committee ($500,000).[REF] California Proposition 25 repealed S.B. 10 by a margin of 56.41 percent to 43.59 percent and effectively restored cash bail.[REF]

That same year, George Gascon was elected Los Angeles County District Attorney, in large part because of an infusion of $2.5 million from George Soros, who gave the money to the California Justice & Safety PAC, which supported Gascon’s campaign.[REF] Upon election, Gascon set up a transition team populated with 38 anti–law-and-order activists and zealots tasked with implementing his progressive (including anti–cash bail) agenda.[REF] This was significant because the Los Angeles County District Attorney’s Office is the largest DA’s office in the country, with upwards of 900-plus prosecutors spread across the massive county. Since one of the hallmarks of the Soros rogue prosecutor movement was to eliminate cash bail, Gascon’s “bail committee” was headed by Robin Steinberg and Dolores Caneles, Community Outreach Director for the Bail Project.[REF]

Moments after Gascon was sworn into office, he issued a blizzard of policy directives. Special Directive 20-06, concerning “Pretrial Release Policy,” claimed that “unaffordable cash bail” creates “a two-tiered system of justice,” leads to “unnecessary incarceration,” and “eviscerates the bedrock of our democracy and undermines our principles of justice, fairness and equality under the law.”[REF] Even though Proposition 25 had just failed, the directive said the D.A.’s Office “will not wait for statewide reform before imposing meaningful changes in the use of cash bail.”[REF] In what has become a mantra for the anti-bail cabal, the directive blurted out that “freedom should be free.”[REF]

The directive does not mention that certain criminals, especially career felons, pose a significant risk of danger to the community and that cash bail is a tool that works to ensure their appearance at future court dates. Nor does the directive mention the law (such as Marsy’s Law), victims’ rights in the California Constitution, or the fact that public safety is and should be the “primary consideration” when setting the appropriate amount of bail.

Gascon’s diktat prohibited his prosecutors from taking into consideration an accused’s criminal history, requesting cash bail for any misdemeanor, and relying solely on scored risk assessment tools for detention.[REF] In a move that likely upset career prosecutors in his office, Gascon’s directive prohibited them from opposing defense motions to remove or modify conditions of release, defense requests to waive client appearances at “non-essential” court proceedings, or defense requests seeking relief from bench warrants for defendants who fail to appear.[REF] In other words, Gascon prevented his prosecutors from being prosecutors.

Other Soros-funded or Soros-inspired California prosecutors such as Chesa Boudin (San Francisco), Pamela Price (Alameda), and Diana Becton (Contra Costa) instituted similar anti-cash bail policies that predictably resulted in rising crime rates in their counties.[REF]

Part III: Humphrey and Kowalczyk

Two years before Proposition 25 repealed S.B. 10 but after S.B. 10 was the law in California, the Court of Appeals for the First District of California held in In Re Kenneth Humphrey that a trial judge (1) failed to make findings and inquiries regarding the financial ability of the defendant to post bail; (2) failed to consider whether less restrictive conditions of release were available; and (3) set bail based solely on the bail schedule rather than on an individualized inquiry.[REF] As a result, the appeals court reversed and remanded the case to the trial court. The trial court conducted a new bail hearing and ordered Humphrey released on various non-financial conditions.

The issue was resolved, and there was no live case or controversy, but that did not stop the California Supreme Court from wading into the issue. Typically, the top appellate court in a state takes cases only where there is a live case or controversy. But not in California, where its Supreme Court injected itself into the bail debate by granting review on its own motion in the Humphrey case. In a 2021 opinion, the court noted that it granted review “to address the constitutionality of money bail as currently used in California as well as the proper role of public and victim safety in making bail determinations.”[REF]

Despite the fact that Humphrey had won his case and obtained the relief he sought, the court held that “where a financial condition is nonetheless necessary, the court must consider the arrestee’s ability to pay the state amount of bail—and may not effectively detain the arrestee solely because the arrestee lacked the resources to post bail.”[REF] The court handed down its decision on March 21, 2021—four months after November 3, 2020, when the voters had soundly rejected Proposition 25 and its idea of eliminating cash bail and replacing it with a scheme based on risk assessment—and did not even mention the results of Proposition 25 in its opinion.

The court noted that in “unusual circumstances” when the “need to protect community safety may conflict with the arrestee’s fundamental right to pretrial liberty…[ to detain an arrestee] a court must find by clear and convincing evidence that no condition short of detention could suffice and then ensure [that] the detention otherwise complies with statutory constitutional requirements.”[REF] If Justice Antonin Scalia had been on the Humphrey court, he would have ridiculed the majority’s “interpretive jiggery-pokery.”[REF]

Courts have no business opining on hypothetical issues or issuing policy statements under the fig leaf of a judicial opinion. Our Founders realized this and embedded limitations on the jurisdiction of United States federal courts in Article III, Section 2, Clause 1 of the U.S. Constitution. As the Supreme Court noted in Flast v. Cohen, the judicial power of the federal courts “is constitutionally restricted to ‘cases’ and ‘controversies.’”[REF] Those two words “have an iceberg quality, containing beneath their surface simplicity submerged complexities which go to the very heart of our constitutional form of government.”[REF] Those two words “limit the business of federal courts to questions presented in an adversary context and in a form historically viewed as capable of resolution through the judicial process.”[REF]

Similar to the federal Constitution, the California Constitution divides the power of the government among the legislative, executive, and judicial branches, but it does not contain a case-or-controversy clause. Under Article VI, Section 12(a), the state’s Supreme Court “may, before decision, transfer to itself a cause in a court of appeal.”[REF] Under Subsection (b), however, the “Supreme Court may review the decision of a court of appeal in any cause.”[REF] The California Supreme Court has interpreted this clause as allowing it to issue advisory opinions, and the “cause” it took up here was obvious: Draft an opinion that discovers a constitutional right to affordable bail and enshrine that right across the state despite the will of the people as recently affirmed by the result of Proposition 25. The court did not cite Section 12 as a justification to render an advisory opinion.

Amicus briefs in support of Humphrey included Chesa Boudin, a San Francisco Deputy Public Defender, various ACLU offices across California, the California Public Defenders Association, and the American Bar Association.[REF] Amicus briefs supporting the People of California included elected district attorneys George Gascon, Summer Stephan, Michael Ramos, and (at least at first) Xavier Becerra, the state’s Attorney General.[REF]

The unanimous decision reads like a policy paper from the Bail Project. The decision notes that an “accused may be impaired to some extent in preparing a defense” and that “studies suggest that pretrial detention heightens the risk of losing a job,” referencing two slanted law review articles critical of pretrial detention, one of which was published by Harvard Law School’s Criminal Justice Program (CJP).[REF]

The two issues not resolved in Humphrey were (1) whether Section 12 of the state constitution “can or should be” reconciled with the public safety bail provision of Marsy’s Law in Section 28(f)(3) and (2) whether it is constitutionally permissible for a trial court to set bail above a defendant’s ability to pay.

The court did not note that the lead author of the Harvard paper was Elana Fogel of the ACLU’s Initiative to Combat Mass Incarceration[REF] or that one of the two faculty co-directors of the CJP was Professor Carol Steiker, a zealous anti–death penalty advocate.[REF] Nor did the court mention the fact that among other people who provided “generous insights and feedback” to the paper were none other than Patrisse Cullors, a radical Marxist, avowed prison abolitionist, and co-founder of Black Lives Matter, and Alec Karakatsanis, a public defender and fellow traveler on the abolish-prisons train.[REF]

To justify its opinion, the court wrote that “principles of equal protection and substantive due process likewise converge in the money bail context. The accused retains the fundamental constitutional right to liberty,” citing the Supreme Court’s opinion in United States v. Salerno.[REF] The problem for the court is that Salerno did not say that. In fact, Salerno held (1) that the federal Bail Reform Act’s authorization of pretrial detention on the basis of future dangerousness constituted a permissible regulation that did not violate substantive due process and was not impermissible punishment before trial and (2) that the Due Process Clause did not categorically prohibit pretrial detention imposed as a regulatory measure on the ground of community danger.[REF] The page in Salerno that the California Supreme Court referenced in its opinion for the proposition that an accused “retains the fundamental right to liberty” was ripped out of context.

In analyzing the Bail Reform Act’s procedures, the U.S. Supreme Court contrasted the “government’s interest in preventing crime,” which it described as “both legitimate and compelling,” with the “other side of the scale…the individual’s strong interest in liberty.”[REF] The Court noted the “importance and fundamental nature of this right” but then added that, “as our cases hold, this right may, in circumstances where the government’s interest is sufficiently weighty, be subordinated to the greater needs of society.”[REF] Nevertheless, the California Supreme Court held in Humphrey that pretrial detention based solely on an accused’s financial condition violates state and federal equal protection and due process principles.[REF]

Those cases set the stage for the latest outrage from the California Supreme Court. In In re Kowalczyk, the court addressed two important questions left unresolved by In re Humphrey: whether Article I, Section 12, or Article I, Section 28(f)(3) of the California Constitution controls the denial of bail in noncapital cases and whether a trial court may set monetary bail above a defendant’s ability to pay. The case arose after Gerald Kowalczyk used several lost credit cards at a fast-food restaurant and was charged with identity theft and related offenses. The allegations themselves were comparatively modest, but Kowalczyk’s record was anything but modest: He had accumulated 64 prior convictions, had failed to comply with supervision while previously released on his own recognizance, and had convictions in multiple counties and states.

The trial court first set bail at $75,000 and later denied bail altogether, citing Kowalczyk’s extensive criminal history, his poor record of compliance, and the substantial risk that he would not appear for future proceedings. While Kowalczyk’s habeas petition was pending, he accepted a plea agreement, received credit for time served, and was released.

The Court of Appeal initially dismissed the petition as moot, but the California Supreme Court transferred the matter back with instructions to decide the constitutional questions. The case thus became the vehicle for resolving a conflict among the Courts of Appeal over the meaning of California’s competing bail provisions and the reach of Humphrey.

The dispute centered on two commands adopted at different times: Section 12’s direction that a person “shall be released on bail,” subject to enumerated exceptions for specified violent or threatening felonies, and Marsy’s Law’s later declaration in Section 28(f)(3) that a person “may be released on bail” and that public safety and victim safety “shall be the primary considerations” when bail is set, reduced, or denied.

The court held that the two provisions could be “harmonized,” but only by treating Section 12 as the controlling limitation on judicial authority. In the court’s view, Section 12 supplies the exclusive categories in which bail may be denied in a noncapital case: certain violent felonies, felony sexual assaults, and other felonies involving threats of great bodily harm, coupled with the constitutionally required findings.

Section 28(f)(3), the court reasoned, does not enlarge those categories. Instead, its use of the word “may” describes only the possibility that a defendant may or may not be released, while its references to public safety, victim safety, and “denying bail” operate only within Section 12’s preexisting framework. Relying on the presumption against implied repeal, the court concluded that Marsy’s Law neither displaced Section 12 nor gave judges broader authority to detain dangerous defendants charged with offenses outside Section 12’s enumerated exceptions.

On the second question, the court held that when preventive detention is unavailable under Section 12, bail generally must be set at an amount reasonably attainable by the particular defendant. A court must first consider whether nonfinancial conditions—such as supervision, monitoring, or required check-ins—can reasonably protect the public, safeguard the victim, and assure the defendant’s appearance. If monetary bail is necessary, the judge must conduct an individualized assessment that includes the seriousness of the offense, the defendant’s criminal record, compliance history, flight risk, danger to others, financial resources, and the availability of less restrictive alternatives. The amount need not be convenient or easily affordable, and a defendant must substantiate a claimed inability to pay. But a judge may not deliberately set an insurmountable figure as an end run around Section 12’s limits on detention.

Although its conclusion may be dressed in the familiar robes of harmonization, the court once again managed to find a creative way to write around what the people enacted. Marsy’s Law did not say that public safety and victim safety should merely be considered after judges had exhausted the narrow detention categories contained in Section 12. It declared that a person “may” be released on bail; expressly governed the “setting, reducing, or denying” of bail; and commanded that public safety and victim safety “shall be the primary considerations.” Those words were adopted against a well-developed constitutional history in which the difference between “shall” and “may” had already carried unmistakable significance. Rather than give the voters’ chosen language its ordinary force, the court converted “may” into a vague statement of abstract possibility and reduced the public-safety mandate to a procedural appendage. That is not faithful harmonization. It is judicial revision.

The court’s reliance on the presumption against implied repeal also obscures the central issue. No one needed to prove that Marsy’s Law silently erased every word of Section 12. The task was to give meaningful effect to both provisions, including the later amendment’s explicit reference to denying bail and its elevation of safety above all other considerations.

A genuinely harmonious reading would recognize that Section 12 preserves a general right to bail while Section 28(f)(3) grants courts discretion to deny it when compelling public-safety or victim-safety concerns demand that result. Instead, the court treated the older provision as a ceiling on the newer one, leaving the operative language of Marsy’s Law with remarkably little independent work to do. The result is especially difficult to defend in a case involving a defendant with 64 prior convictions, repeated supervision failures, and a demonstrated history spanning multiple jurisdictions—the very sort of record that makes a categorical refusal to recognize broader judicial discretion appear detached from practical reality.

Just as troubling is the court’s dismissive treatment of amici curiae on the other side, especially the California District Attorneys Association. The CDAA’s amicus brief made the straightforward textual point that Section 28(f)(3)’s express reference to “denying bail” must do real work; otherwise, the phrase is substantially drained of meaning. The court brushed that argument aside as “unpersuasive,” reasoning that “denying” bail refers only to defendants who are already eligible for detention without bail under Section 12. But that answer assumes the very conclusion the amici challenged.

It does not explain why the voters added broader, later language addressing the denial of bail, used “may” instead of “shall,” and made public and victim safety primary if they intended no meaningful expansion of judicial authority.

The irony is difficult to miss. The amici’s arguments were grounded in the constitutional text, the history of California’s competing bail provisions, the structure of Marsy’s Law, and the electorate’s unmistakable demand that victims and public safety come first. The California Supreme Court’s contrary logic depends on redefining ordinary words, minimizing later-enacted language, and insulating its preferred result behind an unusually rigid application of the rule against implied repeal.

Having disconnected its analysis from the text and from the voters’ evident purpose, the court then pronounced the more natural reading “unpersuasive.” Once again, the people spoke through the initiative process, and once again, the court found a creative way to tell them that the words they enacted do not mean what they plainly say.

Conclusion

The California Supreme Court’s outrageous decision in In re Kowalczyk cannot be allowed to stand. Once again, the court has mistaken judicial invention for constitutional interpretation, elevating a newly minted right to “reasonably attainable” bail over the text, structure, and history of California’s constitution—and over the repeated commands of the people themselves. A constitution that makes public safety and victim safety the “primary considerations” cannot fairly be read to require judges to price dangerous or chronically noncompliant defendants out of detention.

The response should be immediate and sustained. Congress should hold oversight hearings that examine the consequences of judicially imposed “affordable bail” rules, including their effects on interstate crime, federal–state law-enforcement cooperation, victims, repeat offending, and failures to appear. California’s legislature should likewise conduct public hearings; compel testimony from prosecutors, sheriffs, victims, and pretrial-services officials; and require transparent reporting on rearrests, absconding, supervision failures, and crimes committed by defendants released under the new rule.

Most important, Californians should return to the ballot box with a constitutional amendment that leaves no room for further judicial gamesmanship. The amendment should state in plain English that criminal defendants do not have a constitutional right to bail set at an amount they can afford when they are accused of a violent crime, present a serious danger to a victim or the public, pose a substantial flight risk, repeatedly violate release conditions, or have a long criminal record demonstrating chronic recidivism or noncompliance. It should also reaffirm that judges may deny bail in those circumstances and that protection of victims and the public—not a defendant’s financial convenience—remains the primary consideration.

Finally, Californians should use every lawful mechanism of judicial accountability that is available to them. If members of the court persist in rewriting voter-enacted constitutional commands, the public should seriously consider recall proceedings against the responsible justices, consistent with California law and with full respect for due process. Judicial independence is not judicial supremacy. The people are sovereign, and their Constitution is binding. No court is entitled to nullify either one by redefining ordinary words so that they mean their opposite.

Charles D. Stimson is Acting Director of the Legal Policy Center and Senior Legal Fellow, National Security, at The Heritage Foundation.

He got pushed through the airport and was even given a row to himself on the plane to stretch out. Brian Hudson’s December 29, 2024 post about wheelchair preboarding on Southwest Airlines

One of the less discussed benefits to Southwest of assigned seating is lower wheelchair expense. Under open seating, getting on first meant getting first choice of seats. Assigned seating began January 27. Passengers who need assistance still preboard. They just can’t pick someone else’s better seat once they get on.

Southwest Chief Operating Officer Andrew Watterson acknowledged that wheelchair use fell after the change. Back in 2024, Frontier’s Barry Biffle put his airline’s cost at $30–$35 per wheelchair request. As one commenter put it:

It’s amazing how much healthier Southwest’s passengers have become since they switched to assigned seating. 🤔

But Watterson said the remaining wheelchair passengers were now scattered throughout the cabin, which takes longer to manage than seating them near the front. Southwest was calling customers to offer moves into blocked seats farther forward. So that means still getting a free premium seat.

There are still reasons someone might request assistance they don’t need, too. Depending on the airport, it can mean shorter check-in, security or immigration queues, someone to guide you through the terminal, and early access to overhead bin space. Several people say they book wheelchairs for relatives who need help finding gates or dealing with a language barrier. In Beijing a better approach is just the airprot’s escort service.

My biggest objection has always been what this does to someone who actually needs help. There are only so many chairs and, more importantly, only so many people available to push them. When you claim a wheelchair you don’t need, someone else gets left waiting at the gate longer after the flight has deplaned because an attendant is busy giving you a free ride.

Danielle Frankel Reimagines the 21st Century Bridal Store

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After nearly 10 years in business, fashion designer Danielle Frankel Hirsch has opened a 12,000-square-foot location in Tribeca with interiors set to change the bridal game. To create the new space, which opened in early November, Frankel Hirsch worked with AD PRO Directory designer Augusta Hoffman and Studio Partners Architecture.

The nearly two-year design process is complete, and the Danielle Frankel bridal salon is now ready for appointments. “We’re finally at the point where my heart and my head have been,” says Frankel Hirsch, who worked with Hoffman on her previous Los Angeles boutique and New York City atelier. “Because this is our third space together, we can almost speak in shorthand now.”

Frankel Hirsch on the staircase wearing a shirt she designed from Fortuny fabric. Hoffman used the same Fortuny print throughout the space for wrapping side tables, keepsake boxes on the shelves, as well as for the woven seats on barstools.

The unassuming entrance to Danielle Frankel on Hudson Street

In the reception room just inside the street entrance, the desk is custom, the chairs are antique, and the custom pendant is by Jamb.

Hoffman worked with the artist Miriam Ellner to create eglomise paneling surrounding the staircase. “The eglomise is one of my favorite things because when we were working with the artist we worked so intimately with her,” Frankel Hirsch says. “We worked on the placement the way that we do our laces. We kept going back and forth with the drawings because I wanted it to feel like our lace placement.”

On a recent morning, a bride and her wedding party were busy in the VIP suite making a final selection. The beaming group entered the space through an unassuming brown storefront entrance on Harrison Street, its two large glass windows encased in a sheer linen. From there they ascended a staircase up to a large gallery with over 30-foot floor-to-ceiling windows overlooking the streets below on two sides, and a hand-painted mural pattern above on the ceiling.

The interiors of the original 19th-century Queen Anne–style red brick building were transformed by Hoffman (full disclosure: I am writing Hoffman’s design monograph publishing with Rizzoli in Fall 2027) into a modern, elegant bridal boutique that honors the history of the original New York Mercantile Exchange, first established in the 1880s to keep the standards of the grocer business. “We wanted the space to stay pretty bright and focus on the architecture, but we needed a lot of seating,” Hoffman says. “There was a lot of pressure because we wanted it to feel like Danielle and we wanted to leave our mark on the space, but it’s also so beautiful as is and we didn’t want to have too many interventions that take away from that.”

The rug, vintage Dutch leather armchairs, and custom clothing racks create one of the four seated zones in the gallery area. The wall features antique mottled glass windows. “We wanted this wall to feel historically accurate,” Hoffman says.

The custom oak screen features shelves for displaying flower girl dress by Danielle Frankel. The shelves are lined in silver leaf. The antique stool is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric.

The view of the gallery area from a second floor bridal suite. The vintage teak seating set is reupholstered in a fabric by Manuel Canovas and the glass cocktail tables and Fortuny wrapped side tables are custom. The custom window panels are by Augusta Hoffman Studio for Wovn Home and the custom curtain hardware are bronze leaves Hoffman. Hoffman pulled the leaf design from the details on the columns.

What previously functioned as a large, open space with a small mezzanine, had little existing infrastructure–no electrical, HVAC, or plumbing—and is now an area where brides can plot as they make the most important decision for their wedding day. In the gallery, Hoffman created distinct zones where wedding parties wait before their appointment in a private fitting room.

Much of the furniture was sourced by Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch together on trips to Paris. Antique glass vitrines separate the space, which they included from the previous New York gallery and filled with Frankel Hirsch’s own family wedding photos as well as printed details brides can use for their fitting. One vitrine has a board layered with references of crinoline and interlining. Another has custom Danielle Frankel cufflinks. An oak folding screen lines one half of the room, with flower girl dresses set on shelves lined in silver leaf paper. Custom parchment and cerused oak racks Hoffman designed to withstand the weight of a wedding dress line the floor displaying roughly 65 dresses from Danielle Frankel collections.

The reflection of the wall is seen in the mirrored structure designed to house the elevator bank and stairwell to the street entrance. A framed antique floral plaster relief is flanked with 1930s Italian wall sconces in patinated glass and metal.

The staircase and elevator bank within the mirrored structure. “We had to add the elevator for function and for code, and we were like ‘How do we take this existing architecture and put an elevator in the middle of the room?’” Hoffman says. “It feels super unexpected, versus trying to hide it, and you get to see more of the surrounding architecture and light in the reflection.”

The custom rug is topped with an antique stool upholstered in a Fortuny fabric, a 1950s chair, and a 19th-century English pine console table.

The view from the sitting area adjacent to a bar. The vintage teak seating set was among the first items Hoffman and Frankel Hirsch sourced for the space. The custom bar stool is woven with a Fortuny fabric.

The view from within the staircase to the gallery area. The custom center table is by Rafael Carneiro Triboli and the French chairs are from the 1930s.

Hoffman and Studio Partners Architects took what was formerly the mezzanine, originally separated from the below with only a single-bar metal railing, and made it into a full wall with antiqued mottled glass windows. In the center they constructed a cascading grand staircase, cleverly concealing the stockroom underneath, which leads upstairs to three private bridal suites. The hallways, painted in Wenge by Benjamin Moore, lead to the bridal suites, each designed differently and filled with antique and vintage furniture, custom carpets, lighting, and racks. The walls are painted in Presidente Roman Clay by Portola Paints, an earthy contrast to the white and ivory dresses, which are naturally the center of attention for visitors in each room.

When guests are finished in their suites, there’s a bar in the gallery space where wedding parties can sit and make the final selection. The wedding party that morning had selected the dress and were toasting champagne to celebrate. “It’s just as much about the hospitality and the warmth as it is about the gowns,” Frankel Hirsch says.

In the VIP dressing room, which can be accessed through a discreet entrance on Harrison Street, the custom sofa is upholstered in Holland & Sherry’s Corbel in Henna. The artwork features fragments of 19th century Japanese brocade.

The midcentury armchairs are upholstered in a Watts 1874 fabric and flank a vintage oak table.

The rack, mirror, and ceiling pendant are all custom in the VIP bridal suite.

In the dressing area of the VIP bridal suite, the ceiling pendant is custom and the closet wall covering is by Watts 1874.

The 1930s lounge chair is reupholstered in a Fortuny fabric and the 1970s lamp is from Brazil.

Mauricio Pochettino defends decision to stay with USMNT before starting Man United job

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Mauricio Pochettino’s first match as Manchester United manager will be against Liverpool at Anfield

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Mauricio Pochettino says nothing will distract him from the United States job before he leaves it, as he prepares for his final match in charge of the American side on Tuesday.

The US men's national team (USMNT) coach signed a contract to lead the Americans through to the 2030 World Cup after this summer’s tournament, but last week he reached an agreement to replace Michael Carrick at Manchester United.

His first game with the Premier League club will come quickly, with the team due to face arch-rivals Liverpool at Anfield on Sunday after he joins them on Wednesday.

Pochettino's choice to remain with the US for their Concacaf fixture against Haiti has drawn criticism, but the Argentine has defended his decision.

"For me, it's not difficult. I think we are well prepared to switch quick - like a player, from the national team to the clubs. From one game that you don't play well to another that you need to be ready again. From one action to the next action," Pochettino told reporters ahead of the US match in Cincinnati.

"The business today is to be focusing here in Cincinnati for the game against Haiti tomorrow. But for sure, in the moment that (we) finish that game, we are going to start to be 100% focused on our next project."

Manchester United are bidding to climb out of the relegation zone with Pochettino after a disastrous start to the season.

Pochettino will hope to turn their fortunes around after leading the US to considerable success over the last two years.

Under him, the Americans reached the 2025 Gold Cup final, where Mexico beat them. He also steered the US to the last-16 of the World Cup this year on home soil beforr they were beaten by Belgium.

Pochettino explained that members of his coaching staff have already been sent ahead to Manchester to prepare for his arrival later this week.

"That was the plan when we agreed terms with Manchester United and with the national team," he said. "They are preparing all there because Wednesday, when we will arrive, it's going to be non-stop."

Pope Endures Grueling Travel in South America Weeks After Surgery

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

When doctors operated on Pope Leo XIV’s lung in October, some wondered if he’d postpone a long-planned trip to South America. It’s gone well so far.

Motoko Rich, the Rome bureau chief, has been traveling with Pope Leo XIV through Uruguay, Argentina and Peru.

Flying with Pope Leo XIV over the Andes, halfway through the pontiff’s 12-day trip to South America, the journalists traveling at the back of the plane suspected something was amiss.

They had expected the pope to come out from behind the business-class curtain and take questions, as he had on previous papal flights. Instead, Vatican officials abruptly told the press corps that the pope would not be speaking with them on the nearly five-hour flight from Argentina to Peru.

Was the pope, who had been diagnosed with lung cancer less than a month earlier, feeling ill? Was he too exhausted to talk? Was he taking a nap?

“It’s evident he’s well,” said Maria Montserrat Alvarado, who heads the Vatican’s communications department, when asked why the pope wasn’t taking questions. “He’s running circles around us.”

It was perhaps inevitable that Leo’s every action on this journey, so soon after surgery to remove a lobe of his right lung, would be scrutinized for signs of fatigue or illness, particularly as the Vatican is not known for its transparency.

The three-country trip, which began in Uruguay and concludes in Peru on Monday, involves a grueling schedule of 12 flights, two helicopter rides, nine Masses, 36 speeches and numerous meetings with local bishops and community members.

Having a revocable living trust helps avoid probate if assets are titled to the trust, or if assets are left to the RLT via TODs. For all other household type goods they may be able to be moved to the RLT before death via a general assignment of personal property to a revocable living trust. It’s basically a document you sign that moves all typical tangible household type property into the RLT, with perhaps some exceptions (house, cars, etc)

A pourover will won’t avoid probate, but it would help direct straggling assets to the correct eventual beneficiary as designated by the RLT.

Countdown: Midseason managerial appointments from worst to best

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Manchester United have endured their worst start to a season in four decades, dating back to November 1986, when Sir Alex Ferguson replaced Ron Atkinson in the Old Trafford dugout. It would prove to be one of the greatest midseason managerial appointments in football history, with Ferguson going on to deliver 13 league titles and two Champions League crowns before retiring in 2013.

Since then, however, United have suffered a staggering decline, cycling through managers, squandering hundreds of millions in the transfer market and gradually slipping from perennial title contenders to relegation strugglers. Their downfall has reached a new low in November 2026, with the Red Devils languishing in the bottom three of the Premier League.

Now, United are turning to Mauricio Pochettino in the hope that the Argentine can steady the ship and steer them away from the relegation zone. But if the post-Ferguson era has taught us anything, it's that changing managers midway through a season is no guarantee of a turnaround. With that in mind, we're ranking Manchester United's eight midseason managerial appointments since Ferguson's retirement, from worst to best.

Darren Fletcher was tasked with restoring some semblance of stability following the collapse of Ruben Amorim's tenure, but his brief spell in the dugout did little to stop United's downward spiral. The former Scotland international oversaw a draw at Burnley and an FA Cup defeat to Brighton before handing over the reins to Michael Carrick.

Fletcher's tenure was too short to make a lasting impact, but it nevertheless epitomized the chaos engulfing Old Trafford, with United desperately searching for answers to problems that extended far beyond the touchline.

Two years ago, Manchester United splashed out €11 million to prise Ruben Amorim away from Sporting CP, hoping the Portuguese tactician could replicate the success that had transformed him into one of Europe's most coveted young managers.

After ending Sporting's 19-year league title drought and guiding them to another Portuguese championship in 2024, Amorim arrived in England with a reputation for tactical innovation and an unwavering commitment to his 3-4-3 system. However, his methods failed to translate to the Premier League, with United slumping to a 15th-place finish in 2024/25 and losing the UEFA Europa League Final.

Amorim departed with a record of 24 wins, 18 draws and 21 defeats in 63 matches across all competitions, giving him a win percentage of just 38%. It was the lowest of any permanent Manchester United manager since Ferguson's retirement, with only interim boss Ralf Rangnick (37%) faring worse.

Rather than ushering in a new era of success, Amorim's appointment plunged United deeper into the wilderness.

Few figures have left a greater imprint on modern German and Austrian football than Ralf Rangnick. From transforming Hoffenheim into a Bundesliga force to laying the foundations for RB Leipzig's meteoric rise and reshaping Red Bull Salzburg's recruitment strategy, Rangnick built his reputation as one of European football's foremost tactical innovators.

Unfortunately for United, the man dubbed the "Godfather of Gegenpressing" proved incapable of implementing his philosophy at Old Trafford.

Appointed interim manager in November 2021 following Ole Gunnar Solskjaer's dismissal, Rangnick struggled to instill his high-intensity pressing approach in an underperforming squad. United eventually limped to a sixth-place finish, recording their lowest Premier League points tally at the time.

Rangnick had initially agreed to remain at United in a consultancy role for another two years, potentially giving him the opportunity to reshape the club's recruitment and sporting structure. Instead, he departed in the summer of 2022 to focus on managing Austria, leaving behind another failed experiment at Old Trafford.

Less than a year after celebrating their 13th Premier League title under Ferguson, Manchester United found themselves stumbling through one of the most disastrous title defenses in their history.

David Moyes had been handpicked to succeed his fellow Scotsman, but his reign unraveled spectacularly, prompting United to dismiss him in April 2014 and hand the reins to club legend Ryan Giggs.

The Welshman stepped into the unfamiliar role of interim player-manager, overseeing two wins, one draw and one defeat in United's final four matches. Although he briefly lifted the mood at Old Trafford, Giggs was unable to salvage a season that ended with United finishing seventh and missing out on European qualification.

His appointment was ultimately little more than a temporary solution, with Louis van Gaal arriving that summer to spearhead the club's next rebuild.

After spending 12 trophy-laden years at Old Trafford, Michael Carrick hung up his boots in 2018 and transitioned into coaching, working under Jose Mourinho and Ole Gunnar Solskjaer before being thrust into the managerial hot seat following the latter's dismissal in November 2021.

Despite his lack of senior managerial experience, Carrick made an immediate impression. He guided United to a Champions League victory at Villarreal, secured a hard-fought draw at Chelsea and oversaw a thrilling 3-2 win against Arsenal, ending his three-match tenure unbeaten.

Nevertheless, Carrick stepped aside to make way for Ralf Rangnick, whose subsequent struggles only strengthened the argument that United should have allowed their former midfielder to see out the campaign.

It would take another four years for Carrick to receive a second opportunity in the Old Trafford dugout.

Eighteen years after departing Manchester United as one of the most prolific strikers in the club's history, Ruud van Nistelrooy returned to Old Trafford in the summer of 2024, joining Erik ten Hag's coaching staff.

However, with United enduring another miserable start to the season, Ten Hag was dismissed in October, paving the way for Van Nistelrooy to take charge on an interim basis. The Dutchman wasted no time making his mark, overseeing two victories against Leicester City, a Europa League triumph over PAOK and a draw against Chelsea. United scored 11 goals in his four matches, offering a glimpse of the attacking fluency that had been sorely lacking under Ten Hag.

Van Nistelrooy departed unbeaten after Amorim's arrival, having done enough to convince Leicester City to appoint him as their next manager. His brief return to Old Trafford also served as a reminder of how former players can return to their clubs in new roles, a journey that the next generation of footballers will be hoping to emulate. Among those looking to forge their own legacy is LAFC youngster Jude Terry, who has already made the leap from the academy to the first team while helping LAFC2 reach the MLS NEXT Pro Cup Final.

When Michael Carrick returned to the Old Trafford dugout in January 2026, Manchester United were languishing in seventh place, with their hopes of Champions League qualification hanging in the balance. What followed was one of the most remarkable midseason revivals of the post-Ferguson era.

Carrick revitalized a struggling squad, restored confidence to a disillusioned dressing room and transformed United into one of the Premier League's most formidable teams. The Red Devils surged up the standings, eventually finishing third and securing their return to Europe's elite competition.

His achievements earned widespread praise and convinced United's hierarchy to hand him the permanent position. However, the honeymoon period proved painfully short-lived. Carrick struggled to build on his initial success, with United suffering a disastrous start to the 2026/27 campaign before his dismissal.

Nevertheless, his first six months in charge remain one of the most successful short-term managerial spells United have enjoyed since Ferguson's retirement.

Few managerial appointments have captured the imagination of Manchester United supporters quite like Ole Gunnar Solskjaer's return in December 2018.

Having scored the dramatic winning goal in the 1999 Champions League Final, Solskjaer was already immortalized in Old Trafford folklore. Nearly two decades later, he returned as interim manager following Jose Mourinho's dismissal, inheriting a fractured dressing room and a team that had lost its identity.

The Norwegian's impact was immediate. United won his first eight matches across all competitions and went unbeaten in his first 12 Premier League fixtures, with Solskjaer restoring the attacking freedom and confidence that had been missing during Mourinho's final months.

His crowning achievement came in March 2019, when an injury-ravaged United side overturned a 2-0 first-leg deficit against Paris Saint-Germain to reach the Champions League quarterfinals. Marcus Rashford's stoppage-time penalty completed one of the most extraordinary European comebacks in the club's history and helped convince United to appoint Solskjaer permanently.

However, the fairytale eventually lost its magic. United's form deteriorated toward the end of the season, and they finished sixth, missing out on Champions League qualification.

Solskjaer would subsequently guide United to consecutive top-three finishes before being dismissed in November 2021, but his initial resurgence remains arguably the most electrifying managerial bounce United have experienced in the post-Ferguson era.

More than anything, his appointment offered a reminder of what Manchester United had once been: a club defined by attacking football, dramatic comebacks and an unshakable belief that anything was possible.

6 Thriller Shows That Hook You in the First Scene

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Chris is a UK-based film and TV writer, editor and occasional filmmaker. For more than a decade, he has written news, reviews and features covering movies, television and pop culture. He has also made short films and worked in video production, giving him experience on both sides of the screen. Chris runs the We Love Movies More Than You TikTok channel and has a particular interest in overlooked and short-lived TV shows.

Spoiler Alert: This list contains spoilers for multiple shows.TV thrillers have a massive advantage over their film counterparts because they have far more time to build complex stories and take characters to unexpected places. However, that extra time cuts both ways, as a series needs to convince its audience it is worth the investment of hours before it has earned any of them.

The TV shows on this list skillfully use their opening scenes to create an immediately gripping reason to keep watching. Whether it’s a cyberattack on a train or a shocking turn of events at a comic book store, these thrillers waste no time hooking you into their stories full of twists, turns and an overcoat in dire need of dry cleaning.

The first small-screen version of James Patterson's Detective Alex Cross forges its own path by not directly adapting any of the author's books. Instead, Cross tells original stories with the established characters, which may disappoint some fans. However, Ben Watkins serves up a tightly paced psychological thriller that finds Aldis Hodge's commanding Cross on the hunt for a calculating serial killer while he's still emotionally reeling from his wife's unsolved murder.

The first scene shows Alex enjoying time off with his wife, Maria (Chauntee Schuler Irving), and friends, which, as in any crime thriller, immediately raises concerns that things won’t end happily. Even knowing that something bad is likely to happen doesn't soften the shock when an unseen gunman shoots Maria, and she dies in Alex's arms. While it's far from the most original narrative device, it's effectively used, showing us what he's lost and what drives his often reckless decisions. Alex Cross might be a brilliant detective and psychologist who solves disturbing crimes, but he can't resolve his own grief until he finds the killer.

A routine overnight journey from Glasgow to London becomes a dangerous race against time in Nick Leather's real-time thriller Nightsleeper. A hijacked train turns into a weapon after a cyberattack leaves the passengers on board with less than six hours before it reaches its final destination. Police officer Joe Roag (Joe Cole), with remote help from cybersecurity specialist Abby (Alexandra Roach), attempts to regain control of the train and prevent a national disaster.

A seemingly random act of heroism introduces us to Joe. As he's about to board his train, he sees a young mum being mugged and gives chase through the carriages. It's a thrilling sprint through narrow corridors that does more than give us a character to root for or launch the series with urgency. As the story unfolds, we learn that nothing about Joe's chance encounter was random, and his willingness to do the right thing is put to the ultimate test. It's inescapable that to enjoy Nightsleeper fully, you must suspend a fair amount of disbelief, but it's an addictively watchable series from start to finish.

Dennis Kelly's exceptional conspiracy thriller isn't a show for the faint of heart, with its bursts of strong violence and steadily oppressive streak. Strangers who met in an online forum find themselves in possession of a rare unpublished book that a secret organization called The Network will do anything to obtain. The vivid world of Utopia asks a great many questions throughout its short yet compelling run, but none strike as much fear as the first: "Where is Jessica Hyde?"

The series opens with two men, Arby (Neil Maskell) and Lee (Paul Ready), entering a small comic book store to ask about a fabled sequel manuscript to The Utopia Experiments. The staff members are rightly cautious, and the atmosphere in the room tightens with every exchange as it becomes clear that the pair are not comic book fans. Arby repeatedly asks, "Where is Jessica Hyde?" and when they fail to answer, everyone in the store meets a grim end. It's a disturbing start to a series that routinely caused controversy for its violence, and it sets the precedent that in Utopia, nobody is safe.

Britain makes more detective television than any country has any business making, and somehow almost all of it is good. Here is the one you belong in, what it says about you, and the case for it being the best of the four.

You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

The jewel in Apple TV's crown, this spy series based on the books by Mick Herron has not dropped in quality one bit since it launched in 2022, which is high praise after six seasons. Gary Oldman leads the cast as jaded spymaster Jackson Lamb, relegated to running Slough House, the place where British intelligence dumps its disgraced or burnt-out operatives. Unlike many espionage shows, Slow Horses takes a grittier and unexpectedly funny approach, with the first episode setting the tone before Mick Jagger starts singing the theme music.

We are dropped straight into an unfolding operation, with agent River Cartwright (Jack Lowden) in the field at Stansted Airport. MI5's second-in-command, Diana Taverner (Kristin Scott Thomas), oversees from Regent's Park, with arrogant agent Webb (Freddie Fox) on comms. Acting on bad information from Webb, River tears through the airport to apprehend the bomber, but he's been chasing the wrong man and the real target gets away. It's only then do we find out the whole thing was a training exercise, one that River has spectacularly and publicly failed. An immediate way to hook us into the deceitful, grubby world of Slow Horses, where blame always travels downwards.

Based on Frederick Forsyth's book, The Day of the Jackal gets its first television adaptation from Top Boy creator Ronan Bennett. In this version, Eddie Redmayne is cast strikingly against type as the elusive contract killer known as the Jackal. He becomes relentlessly pursued by MI6 agent Bianca Pullman (Lashana Lynch), locking them into a volatile cat-and-mouse game as he attempts to pull off the most ambitious kill of his career. The high-stakes world of the Jackal becomes clear from the opening scene. With barely a line of dialogue spoken, the thrilling sequence shows how his cold, clinical detachment makes him the best at what he does.

In a quiet, dimly lit room, an older man methodically prepares for work. Using an effective one-shot, he shuffles through his apartment, which reveals a murdered man who looks just like him. The disguised Jackal uses the dead man's identity to get into the building of his intended target, Elias Fest, the son of an affluent German politician. Moving through the building, he kills multiple people along the way; when he finds Elias, the Jackal only wounds him, as the real mission was to lure out Elias' father. It's a highly efficient opening that instantly shows how far the Jackal will go and sets the pace for the series that unfolds.

Jed Mercurio's tense miniseries stars Richard Madden as former soldier-turned-police protection officer David Budd. His time in Afghanistan left him with untreated PTSD, and his new assignment guarding Home Secretary Julia Montague (Keeley Hawes) is proving complicated as he holds her and her government responsible for the war that cost so many people everything. Opposing ideologies are set aside when David and Julia find themselves at the heart of a dangerous conspiracy that began long before their paths crossed.

Bodyguard starts with a nail-biting 20-minute sequence that's usually reserved for a season finale. When we first meet David, he's on a train with his sleeping children, but he can't help noticing something is off. As he searches the carriages, he discovers a terrified woman, Nadia (Anjli Mohindra), wearing an explosive vest and on the verge of pressing the detonator. The pressure increases as the armed police outside want to eliminate the threat; David refuses to let that happen and keeps trying to talk her down in what becomes unbearably stressful to watch. Beyond the claustrophobic setting, the whole opening sequence is brilliantly constructed and becomes equally important in the finale. David proves himself capable and morally driven, but he's visibly broken. Bodyguard spends six episodes pushing a fractured man to his limits.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Lakers almost blow 31-point lead before escaping Phoenix with a victory

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The 31-point lead the Lakers built late in the third quarter looked insurmountable, especially with the tear Luka Doncic was on. But the Lakers learned that no lead is too big in the NBA and the Phoenix Suns were out to show them that.

Though the Lakers began to reel as their lead was sliced to five points late in the fourth quarter, they were able to withstand their own inequities and the onslaught from the Suns to hold on for a 131-123 win over Phoenix on Sunday afternoon.

Doncic led the Lakers with 43 points and nine rebounds and Quentin Grimes had 22, including a bank-in three-pointer with 32.2 seconds left to ice the game and send the Lakers to their eighth straight win.

The Lakers led 101-70 with 1:47 left in the third quarter, the game looking over. But after the Suns cut the lead to nine points in the fourth, Lakers coach JJ Redick called a timeout with 5:02 left.

Read more: While tied for NBA's best record, Lakers believe they can be better

Out of the timeout, Ziaire Williams was called for an offensive foul, leading to a turnover.

Ryan Dunn hit a three-pointer and the Lakers' lead was down to six points.

The Suns got to within 126-121, but after Grimes got an offensive rebound, he eventually scored on the three-ball for the Lakers to escape.

Earlier, Doncic drilled a three-pointer in the face of Suns defensive ace Dillon Brooks and backpedaled down court smirking, his head bobbing and weaving while Phoenix was calling a timeout with 6:57 left in the first quarter.

Even with three days off since the Lakers last played a game, Doncic was still in a groove and in his bag, his first-quarter scoring binges still a real thing.

Doncic had 20 points in the first quarter, going five for 10 from the field, three for seven from three-point range and seven for eight from the free-throw line in the first 12 minutes. He leads the NBA in first-quarter scoring (13.6) and in scoring per game (38.6).

Matisse Thybulle of the Lakers blocks the shot of Devin Booker in the first half Sunday. (Kelsey Grant / Getty Images)

This was just another masterpiece for Doncic, who was serenaded with "MVP" chants from some fans inside Mortgage Matchup Arena after he scored, was fouled and made the free throw for a three-point play with 33.9 seconds left in the third.

Walker Kessler did his thing all game long with a double-double of 14 points and 12 rebounds.

Collin Sexton, who suffered a right orbital laceration in the fourth quarter of Wednesday night's game at Milwaukee, wore protective glasses when he entered the game with 3:58 left in the first quarter and gave the Lakers a lift from that point on. But before the first was over, Sexton had taken the goggles off. Soon after, he scored on a floater while being fouled with 50.1 seconds left in the first. He finished his shift with 16 points.

The Lakers actually began this trip a week ago, starting with three games that began in Detroit and went through Atlanta in a back-to-back game and finishing at Milwaukee last Wednesday.

The Lakers then had three days off before going back on the road in Phoenix.

They came here on a seven-game winning streak and were showing good signs of what Redick wants his team to build along the way.

Read more: Lakers' Austin Reaves considered day-to-day with heel injury

"We all want the good results, but what we're trying to do is build an identity and I think our offense, advantage creation, organization, that stuff has been good," Redick said. "I think that's been reflected in our offensive rating. Still need to do a better job managing the ball. And then defensively for us, I think we're building a real identity of being a disruptive basketball team. Still think we can be better with our shifts, we can be better with our rotations, we can be better at staying out of scrambles.

"But like the disruptiveness is part of who we are and I think that's something we just frankly haven't had since I've been here and it's a big part of what's helping us win."

Lakers guard Austin Reaves missed the game with a left heel contusion/bone bruise. Reaves, who is averaging 24.7 points per game and 4.7 assists, has been "improving" since he was first injured last week in Milwaukee.

"It really comes down to just like a pain tolerance thing," Redick said. "Again, nothing structural beyond a bruise and so I think every day it's been better and we'll see where he's at in 48 hours when we play on Tuesday."

The Lakers play Charlotte on Tuesday at Crypto.com Arena.

Rewards Credit Cards In A Nutshell: What The Biggest Ones Are Actually Good For (And Where They Fall Short)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I receive compensation for content and many links on this blog. Be aware that websites may earn compensation when a customer clicks on a link, when an application is approved, or when an account is opened. Citibank is an advertising partner of this site, as is American Express, Chase, Capital One, and others. Any opinions expressed in this post are my own, and have not been reviewed, approved, or endorsed by my advertising partners. I do not write about all offers that are available -- instead focusing on miles, points, and cash back (and currencies that can be converted into the same). Terms apply to the offers and benefits listed on this page.

There are tons of in-depth reviews of rewards credit cards. The truth is that the value proposition of each can be boiled down to essentials. We can talk about what each one does well – and does poorly – in a nutshell. That helps understand all of the details without getting lost, and gives a framework for thinking about each of the pieces that get pretty complicated.

Here’s how I think about several of the major cards, boiled down to their essentials:

Chase Sapphire Reserve® (See rates and fees) This is a good lounge card, a good spending card, but it’s expensive and the coupons aren’t nearly as great as Amex Platinum.

You can make the economics of the card work, especially if you can do high spend in bonus categories – 4x on direct airfare spend, 4x on hotel spend, 8x on Chase Travel portal spend and 3x on restaurants and dining is great.

I’ve put over $75,000 which earns me an additional $500 Southwest Airlines credit booking through Chase’s portal; Southwest’s A-List status; IHG Diamond status and a $250 Shops at Chase credit on top of the points from the spend. And I use the The Edit credits (2 x $250 on minimum two-night hotel stay) and plan to use the $250 hotel brand-specific credit (which can be stacked with The Edit credit and the $300 travel credit) and easily using the dining credits, so the card really works for me.

American Express Platinum Card® (see rates and fees) The most expensive mainstream card with an $895 annual fee, but the credits they give you are worth several times the fee. This is not a good card for spending, outside of airfare. But if you make the credits work, it covers the cost of the card and then some and pays for your lounge access. Amex has the biggest lounge network, but Centurion lounges aren’t as nice as Chase lounges and the food is much worse than Capital One lounges.

Capital One Venture X Rewards Credit Card (See rates and fees) It’s the least expensive of the premium rewards cards, the value premium card. Capital One lounges are the best bank lounges, but there aren’t enough of them. It’s a good card for spending at 2x transferrable points on everything, though the standard $95 annual fee Capital One Venture Rewards Credit Card (See rates and fees) is identical in earn.

The $395 annual fee is easily ‘covered’ by the card’s $300 travel portal credit (I just buy a single airine ticket each year) and the annual 10,000 points at card renewal. Then it gives me lounge access – the JFK lounge especially is great, and the two ‘Landings’ (at LaGuardia and Washington’s National airport) have the best lounge food in the United States. I basically view this access as becoming free given the cards fee vs. credits. It used to be my ‘catch all’ spending card before Bilt introduced its new card products.

Citi Strata EliteSM Card (See rates and fees.) offers transferable points and the only transfers to American Airlines AAdvantage. Their hotel portal earns 12x, and I’ve found favorable pricing. The credits more than cover the card’s annual fee, by a lot in the first year, and that’s an inducement to try it out. While I have it I like earning 6x at restaurants including restaurant delivery services every Friday and Saturday from 6 PM to 6 AM ET.

Bilt Palladium Card (See rates and fees) is a Bilt Points-earning machine.

It has the potential to be a 4x on everything card, but you could ignore every feature the card has and just treat it as ‘2x on everything’ and it’s still earning the most valuable points currency with the best points-transfer partners. Plus, Bilt Rewards has the biggest transfer bonuses, often up to 100%.

The card’s rollout was rocky at the start of the year and so it’s really undervalued now. I’m earning more points than before – a lot more – and then transferring them at 100% bonuses or more (which I’ve taken advantage of with Bilt to Air France KLM, British Airways, Japan Airlines, Virgin Atlantic).

Bilt Blue Card (See rates and fees) is really underappreciated as the best no annual fee in the market. It earns the most valuable transferable points, that can be spent through Bilt’s travel portal at 1.25 cents apiece or transferred to airline and hotel partners (more partners than Amex, Chase, Capital One or Citi). And since you can earn up to 1.25X points on rent and mortgage payments by matching the spend you do on the card, you can actually wind up with 2.25 points per dollar on your general spend. More than 2x earn with the best points makes it unbeatable at the price point.

For rates and fees of the American Express Platinum Card®, click here.
