Robinhood gold visa. $50/year. 3% back on everything, 4% on meals Fidelity. 2% back on everything.

@old card user — Fidelity 2% is solid for pure cash back, though in the context of this post, it lacks transferable currency upside (compared to something like Citi Double Cash, which can convert to ThankYou points and transfer to AA if paired with a Strata card).

Robinhood Gold also comes with a lot more friction than “3% on everything” implies. Paying $50/year for Gold means you have to spend $5,000 just to break even against a free 2% card. You only get the full 1 cent per point (3%) if you redeem into a Robinhood brokerage account, their portal, or gift cards—taking a standard statement credit drops the value to 0.7 cents (2.1%). And there’s still a waitlist, they exclude major categories like tax payments and rent, and new accounts now get hit with a 3% foreign transaction fee. And all that’s before factoring in Robinhood’s track record with customers—not exactly an issuer many people trust as their primary daily driver.

High flat-rate cash back cards almost always come with strings or a short shelf life anyway: Bank of America’s Preferred Rewards still gives 2.625%–5.25% back if you’re willing to park $100k+ (no, wait, now $1 million!) with them, while U.S. Bank famously promised 4% on the Smartly card only to nerf it shortly after launch.

Shiba Inu (SHIB), Solana (SOL) and Zcash (ZEC) Price Analysis For November 16: Is This Finally the Recovery Point?

📅 2026-11-16  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Solana is the most vulnerable, trading at $71.89 below all major moving averages, with the $60 level as the closest support.

Zcash looks stronger, holding the 200 EMA at $915 after a 6% daily gain, though it needs to reclaim $1,000 to avoid a deeper correction. Shiba Inu has lost its July low of $0.00000413 and trades near $0.00000391 with RSI around 25, so a short-term retrace is possible, but bears on the market prevail

Shiba Inu has been struggling on the market for ages, and November 14 made things worse: SHIB broke below its July low of $0.00000413 and dipped to around $0.0000037.

SHIB mostly followed the uptrend from July to October, but tumbled quickly due to the general reversal of the cryptocurrency market. The bounce from the July level was unlikely to occur naturally, and the zone where some buy orders might have been concentrated didn't hold.

Back in September, the 200 EMA acted as strong resistance that SHIB was unable to break, and the asset has been sliding ever since. The ascending trendline from July was lost back in October.

SHIB is trading at $0.00000391, bouncing slightly from the low. The drop below $0.000004 isn't a guarantee of anything, as SHIB has already been lower in previous years. RSI is moving around 25, deep in the oversold zone. Multiple indicators suggest a retrace, but the overall market picture is grim, to say the least.

According to CryptoQuant, SHIB held on exchanges is worth around $332 million, while the netflow stays negative. In tokens, around 86 trillion SHIB still sit on exchanges. Most of the activity is still most likely fueled by retail investors, as the largest players stay sidelined or are not exposed to SHIB at all.

Shiba Inu volumes are still low, which suggests a lack of volatility ahead. The market is weak, and it might get weaker, though most of the selling pressure SHIB could face has most likely been injected already.

Solana is clearly struggling, and the death cross on the chart is not likely to help it push higher. The volume uptick reinforces the bearish narrative and could push the price even lower. The volume on November 14 was the highest since early September.

SOL is trading at $71.89 after dipping to $67.37, around 42% below its September peak. The correction is rapidly erasing the rally Solana has built since August, and the price is already back in the range last seen in July.

Solana trades below all major moving averages, with most of them pointing down, which signals that the current trend is getting worse. RSI is beyond oversold, and it is not clear where the selling pressure is coming from. We also saw additional injections of capital from whales, so the current downtrend is mostly fueled by capitulation of larger players, though not by institutions.

The death cross we are witnessing on the EMAs is important, but barely significant for the asset itself. It is a mere reflection of market sentiment, rather than anything else. The short-term moving average is falling rapidly, which suggests an extremely negative short-term dynamic.

The blue moving average crossed below the black one at around $96, roughly 25% above the current price. Realistically, the closest "fair" support is the $60 price level, where Solana bounced back in June.

A better time to buy Solana could be tied to an overall market recovery, which needs a stronger market catalyst that does not exist at this point. Unfortunately, there are little to no fundamental factors that can help Solana reach key market levels right now.

Zcash rapidly dived toward the 200 EMA, and the downtrend isn't going anywhere. However, the situation is very different from Solana. A solid support still exists for the price, and the situation is not as dire.

Zcash has a strong foundation, which acts as a bouncing pad for the asset, even though it lost 46% from the local top, when it traded close to $1,700. ZEC is trading at $915, up almost 6% today after a wick toward $800 on November 14. The longest moving average is still moving below the price, suggesting the long-term uptrend isn't broken yet.

Zcash is bullish and not backing down yet. Privacy is always in demand, even though Zcash has to "compete" with Monero. Monero developers, for instance, are now reviewing a pull request that delays Tor and I2P transaction relay to make transactions harder to trace.

The NU7 update, activated on November 6, also made the Zcash network somewhat healthier by decreasing the target block interval to 25 seconds.

The price violated the $1,000 level, and the moving averages are trying to converge rapidly. The convergence of moving averages might lead to a volatility spike. From the momentum perspective, RSI is close to 30, so the asset is almost oversold.

In order to avoid a deeper correction, Zcash must reclaim the $1,000 price level. If the price violates the 200 EMA, our next stop would be the $800 area, which acts as a psychological threshold rather than a strong technical level.

Why The Longest Single-Aisle Twinjet Ever Built Is An Aircraft Hard For Airlines To Get Rid Of

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

While not as rough as the more recent Airbus A330-800neo, the Boeing 757-300 variant was a disappointment for Boeing. Unfortunately, Boeing does not release figures for the incremental breakeven point needed to cover the variant's development costs, and it is unclear what sales Boeing envisioned when it commissioned the program in the 1990s. However, it is safe to say that it planned to sell a good many more than the 55 examples it did.

It is therefore somewhat paradoxical that one of Boeing's more disappointing variants has also become one of the most difficult to replace. Today, only two major passenger service commercial operators fly this type: United Airlines and Delta Air Lines. In recent years, the two giants have announced plans to replace their fleets of 757-200s and 767-300ERs, but not the 757-300s.

The 757 is a heavy narrowbody airliner with some characteristics typically associated with widebody airliners, such as a double-wheel bogie assembly. It was developed as a mid-sized airplane to bridge the gap between Boeing's narrowbody 737 series and the relatively small 767 widebody. It was produced in two basic variants (excluding freighter and special mission subvariants), the 757-200 and 757-300.

The original 757-200 version entered service with Eastern Air Lines in 1983 and had a full payload range of 3,860 nautical miles. The 757-200 was by far the more popular variant, accounting for 913 units sold, out of the family's total production run of 1,050 aircraft. The 757-300 was the stretched version of the 757 family and entered service with Condor in 1999, making it much newer. With a length of 178.7 feet (54.4 meters), it is notable for being the longest narrowbody airliner ever built.

The 757-300 replaced some older aircraft like the McDonnell Douglas DC-1, had a range of around 3,395 nautical miles, and was surprisingly unpopular. Only 55 units were ever ordered. Customers had the option of ordering the Rolls-Royce RB211-535E4B or the Pratt & Whitney PW2043 turbofans for the aircraft. The type is capable of seating around 234 to 243 passengers in a standard two-class layout and up to 295 in a high-density charter configuration. It also offers almost 2,400 cubic feet of cargo space.

As stated, orders for the stretched 757-300 were disappointing, with just 55 examples ultimately delivered to seven customers. Northwest Airlines had the largest order (16), followed by Condor (13), ATA Airlines (12), Continental Airlines (nine), Arkia (two), Thomas Cook Airlines (two), and Icelandair (one). All these operators (except for Arkia and Thomas Cook) also purchased the 757-200.

Boeing records show that Northwest was the only airline to opt for its 757-300s to be equipped with Pratt & Whitney engines; all the others came with Rolls-Royce engines. This means that of the 55 delivered 757-300s, Rolls-Royce accounted for 39, and Pratt powered the remaining 16. Northwest's aircraft were inherited by Delta Air Lines following the merger.

Data from Planespotters.net show all sixteen ex-Northwest aircraft remain in service with Delta. Meanwhile, Continental subsequently purchased around half of the world's supply of 757-300s on secondary markets, and United currently operates 21 of them. Ukraine's SkyLine Express operates five of them, and Fly Khiva (an Uzbek airline) operates two ex-Icelandair examples. Icelandair eventually took over the two Arkia 757-300s, but has since divested the type.

Boeing has been considering a 'New Midsize Airplane,' commonly known as NMA and the Boeing 797, but it has yet to come to fruition.

The 757 filled a specific niche: the gap between narrowbody and widebody aircraft. The issue was that while there was demand for such an aircraft, it was much smaller than demand for narrowbody 737s and A320neos or larger 767s (later 787s) and A330s. This was made worse after the September 11 attacks and the subsequent slump in air travel, with the resulting drop-off in new airline orders for the aircraft.

Boeing ended production of the 757 in 2004 without replacement. Airbus also didn't offer a mid-sized airplane to fill the gap. In the later 2010s, Boeing began to seriously consider a clean-sheet replacement to fill the gap. Such an aircraft would be able to both attract new orders and replace aging 757s. The peak of Boeing's customer consultations with over 50 airlines came in 2017 and 2018 with its New Midsized Airplane (NMA), or 797 concept.

But then came the 2018 and 2019 MAX crashes, which, together with COVID-19 and other issues, plunged Boeing into a prolonged crisis that it is still recovering from. Dennis Muilenburg (during the Q3 2019 Earnings Call) stated that the NMA remained "a serious project" under evaluation, though he acknowledged Boeing's immediate priority was safely returning the 737 MAX to service before committing capital to a new airplane program. But the NMA never happened; the MAX and 777X remained Boeing's priorities for its strained finances.

Now it appears the window of opportunity for Boeing's NMA has passed. Airbus developed the stretched A321neo and then developed the extended-range A321XLR. This aircraft is already filling much of the mid-sized airplane gap and is well on its way to replacing many of the world's aging 757-200 fleets while also opening up many new thin routes around the world. Widebodies, like the shorter 787-8, cost significantly more to operate per trip.

Making matters worse for any future NMA, JetZero, in partnership with Northrop Grumman, is powering on with its Z4 blended-wing-body aircraft. Should that aircraft actually enter service in the 2030s, it would be targeting the mid-sized market. It has secured investment from some of Boeing's most loyal customers, such as Alaska Airlines.

The A321XLR offers a dramatic improvement in fuel burn compared with the 757 family, but is better suited to replace the 757-200. It directly targets the thin transatlantic and medium-long-haul routes that the 757-200 served. But it is less suited to replacing the 757-300 as a high-capacity domestic/short-to-medium-haul stretcher. The other modern alternative, the delayed 737 MAX 10, also carried fewer passengers in comparable premium-heavy configurations.

There isn't a single airliner that can replicate all of the Boeing 757's capabilities, but perhaps the market doesn't require one.

Hypothetically, in a simplistic world where Airbus thought it could replace all 900+ 757-200s with a special A320 stretch, it could be worth it. This assumes no other customers or market growth. A modern high-end Boeing or Airbus clean-sheet airliner would likely need to sell 800 to 1,500+ aircraft just to break even over a 20- to 30-year production run.

A stretch is very different. As a rough guide, Airbus would likely need to only sell 150-400 incremental units to break even. This assumes no cannibalization of its other aircraft. In this overly-simple scenario, it makes sense for Airbus to develop a special variant (e.g., A321XLR) to replace the 757-200. But the 757-300 is very different. From the narrow point of view of replacing the 757-300, there is almost no market.

Only 55 examples were ever sold; that is not remotely enough for an airplane maker to recoup the costs of developing a stretch or special variant to replace it. In the real world, there would likely be considerable opportunity for growth beyond the remaining operators, but the basic point remains. This is a very small niche. Airbus has already been stung by disappointing programs like the A380 and A330-800neo, which almost certainly lost money. As stated, Boeing considered filling the much broader midsize segment, but ultimately, its core 737, 787, and 777X programs were much more pressing.

With Icelandair retiring its limited fleet of 757-300s, the conversation about replacing the aircraft is basically a conversation about United and Delta. That conversation is also related to the 757's twin, the widebody 767. The two aircraft were built to be as interchangeable as possible, and both United and Delta operate the world's largest 767 fleets. Replacing the 757, therefore, also concerns replacing the 767.

Besides filling specific niches that other aircraft can't and being heavily interchangeable with the 767, the 757-300 has another advantage. Being older, they are already paid off. This means the aircraft can partially compensate for higher maintenance expenses with 767 interchangeability and higher fuel consumption with lower capital/leasing costs.

United and Delta have repeatedly extended the 757-300's service life well into the late 2020s and the early 2030s. Both airlines are well into their efforts to replace their fleets of 757-200s (partially with A321XLRs). United's 757-300 fleet is now an average of 23 years old, while Delta's is closer to 24 years. Neither has announced a set one-for-one replacement for them, as no existing narrowbody matches their combination of high multi-class seating capacity and other profiles.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Is A Priority Pass Airport Lounge Membership Worth It?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you value airport lounge access, odds are that you’re familiar with Priority Pass, which is the world’s largest network of airport lounges. In this post, I’d like to share my take on the current value proposition of a Priority Pass™ membership, including discussing the basics of what this airport lounge network is, how you can get a membership, and whether Priority Pass is worth it.

Priority Pass is the world’s largest independent network of airport lounges, with over 1,900 airport lounges and experiences around the world.

Think of Priority Pass as almost being like a lounge access broker. Priority Pass doesn’t operate lounges itself, but rather the business model is to sell lounge memberships to travelers (either directly or through credit cards), and then in turn, pay lounges for admitting members.

It goes without saying that not all airport lounges participate in Priority Pass. For example, in the United States, no American Admirals Club, Delta Sky Club®, or United Club, participates in Priority Pass. That’s because they’d view joining Priority Pass as cannibalizing their own business of selling lounge memberships, getting people to pursue elite status, etc.

For the lounges that do participate in Priority Pass, the individual lounges are being paid by Priority Pass every time a member visits. You’ll always want to check the Priority Pass app or website to check locations, hours, and any access restrictions. For example:

It has to be acknowledged that since Priority Pass is a network of independent lounges, the quality of them can vary drastically. After all, lounges get paid a fixed amount regardless of the quality, so for many lounges, there’s not really an incentive to go above and beyond.

You’re not going to find any of the world’s top first class or business class lounges in Priority Pass, but some are pretty good quality. In my experience, the further from the United States you get, the more pleasant Priority Pass lounges are:

You can either purchase a Priority Pass™ membership directly, or get one through a premium credit card (with the latter being a much better value). Let’s take a look at the details of those two options.

Priority Pass has three types of memberships you can purchase. Which membership makes most sense for you depends on how often you plan on visiting lounges. The Priority Pass™ membership options are summarized below, including the cost, the number of included visits, and the guesting cost.

As you can see, you’ll be paying around $470 per year for a membership that gets you unlimited lounge visits. This is a bit cheaper than the lounge memberships that most of the major US airlines sell directly, for example. However, airline lounge memberships don’t get you access to Priority Pass lounges, and a Priority Pass™ membership doesn’t get you access to most airline membership lounges.

Just to crunch the numbers on which of these memberships represents the best value, below is a chart comparing the value of the three membership tiers based on how many lounge visits you make over the course of a membership year.

As you can see, when it comes to the value of the three Priority Pass memberships:

It’s also worth emphasizing that if you frequently travel as a couple, it may be worth buying multiple memberships, rather than paying the $35 guesting cost each time. Everyone has to crunch the numbers for themselves.

The much more economical way to get a Priority Pass™ membership is to pick up a premium credit card that offers lounge access as a perk. There are plenty of popular cards that can provide access to participating Priority Pass™ lounges.

Just to give some examples, you get a Priority Pass membership with the American Express Platinum Card® (learn more), Bilt Palladium Card (learn more), Capital One Venture X Rewards Credit Card (learn more), Chase Sapphire Reserve® Card (learn more), and Citi Strata Elite℠ Card (learn more), among other cards (Rates & Fees) (Enrollment required).

All of these memberships allow unlimited visits, and all the cards allow members to bring two guests into Priority Pass lounges with them, with the exception of the Capital One Venture X. It’s kind of astonishing how much more lucrative the credit card route is.

As an example of why this is a better value (if you’re eligible for such a card), let’s take a look at the Capital One Venture X. The card has a $395 annual fee (less than the Prestige Priority Pass™ membership cost) and offers:

Personally I value the $300 travel credit and 10,000 anniversary bonus miles at more than the $395 annual fee, and view it as Capital One just throwing in a access to participating Priority Pass™ lounges for free. 😉

One important restriction to be aware of is that Priority Pass™ access through most American Express, Capital One, Chase, and Citi cards, don’t get you credits at Priority Pass restaurants, as well as some other Priority Pass experiences. So if that’s important to you, you’ll want to pursue lounge access through other means.

There’s no good way to develop an objective valuation of a Priority Pass™ membership, given that it’s entirely dependent on how often you use it. To state the obvious, a Priority Pass™ membership is worth how many times you use participating lounges, multiplied by how much you value each visit.

What complicates things further is that it’s not just a question of how often you’d use airport lounges, but rather how often you’d specifically use Priority Pass lounges:

Even beyond that, there’s so much variability in terms of the value of a particular lounge visit, and that should also impact your valuation, based on the airports you frequent:

I will say that for a frequent traveler who doesn’t otherwise have lounge access, even outright paying for a membership with unlimited visits could make sense (assuming you don’t have access to credit cards that offer a membership).

As someone with oneworld Emerald status, SkyTeam Elite Plus status, and an American Admirals Club membership, who typically flies in first & business class, here’s where I see value with Priority Pass:

If I had to put a number to it, I’d say that for my personal usage patterns, I probably value a Priority Pass™ membership at around $200-300 per year.

At least that’s what I’d probably pay in cash for a membership. I really struggle with the concept of paying $30+ for visiting a typical Priority Pass lounge, as I’d rather just grab something I actually want to eat or drink in the terminal, and finding a quiet place to sit, given how crowded many lounges have become.

Fortunately I don’t actually have to put much thought into this decision, since I have multiple Priority Pass™ memberships through several credit cards, so it costs me nothing extra.

The good news is that most of the credits cards that offer Priority Pass™ memberships also offer access to their own credit card lounge networks, which are often far superior. This includes Amex Centurion Lounges, Capital One Lounges, and Chase Sapphire Lounges, all of which are top notch.

The value of a Priority Pass™ membership will vary significantly depending on the type of traveler you are. If you do want a Priority Pass™ membership, however, you’re almost always going to be better off getting one through a credit card. There are some premium cards that are so valuable that you might not even have to account for the cost of the Priority Pass™ membership with your card annual fee.

Personally I value a Priority Pass™ membership at somewhere around $200 to $300 per year, though everyone will no doubt have a different valuation based on their usage patterns.

What’s your take on the value of Priority Pass, and how much would you pay for a membership?

Membership is free and takes no more information than commenting as a guest. Earn status points for good contributions, and see fewer ads as your status grows.

Japan's economy unexpectedly contracts in third-quarter as elevated energy costs bite

📅 2026-11-15  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Japan's economy unexpectedly contracted in the third quarter, shrinking at an annualized 0.1% compared to the previous quarter, as a slowdown in consumption and exports pressured growth.

Reuters poll had estimated the economy to grow at 0.3% in the three months to September, while GDP had expanded at 1.4% in the previous quarter.

While Japan's exports beat expectations for two of the three months in the quarter, net exports weighed on the economy due to higher energy imports, cutting 0.6 percentage point from the country's growth.

Following the data release, the Nikkei 225 rose 0.4%, while the yield on the benchmark 10-year Japanese Government Bonds was at 2.885%. The yen weakened marginally against the dollar, trading at 159.76.

Inside Baby2Baby’s Star-Studded, Record-Breaking 15th Anniversary Gala

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

On Saturday evening at West Hollywood’s Pacific Design Center, Demi Moore asked a ballroom filled with Hollywood stars to do something unexpected: take two deep breaths. The actress, who was being honored at Baby2Baby’s 15th anniversary gala, wanted everyone to consider the sense of relief that comes when a parent no longer has to worry about providing basic necessities for their child. It was one of the evening’s most moving moments, and a reminder of the mission behind a night that would go on to raise over $20 dollars—the highest fundraising total for the nonprofit to date.

Presented by Paul Mitchell, the annual fundraiser returned, bringing together celebrities, philanthropists, and longtime supporters. The next-level guest list included Katy Perry and Justin Trudeau, Jessica Alba, Kerry Washington, Ciara, Chrissy Teigen, Paris Hilton, Sofía Vergara, Elsa Hosk, and Miranda Kerr, all gathering to celebrate an organization that has spent the past 15 years providing essential items to children across the country.

Before the big night got underway, guests were treated to a luxe dining experience featuring 20 chefs and endless options. Jon Shook and Vinny Dotolo oversaw the hors d’oeuvres, which included tuna tartare and crispy rice with avocado, before attendees made their way to a dining area filled with stations from some of the most sought-after restaurants in Los Angeles and beyond. Carbone served its signature spicy rigatoni vodka, KazuNori rolled fresh hand rolls, Funke offered cannelloni bolognese, and Matū dished out Philly cheesesteaks en masse. Ensuring something for everyone, there were also oysters from Found Oyster, brisket from Moo’s Craft Barbecue, and crowd-pleasers by Mr. Chow, Holy Basil, and Saffy’s.

Once everyone settled in, Keke Palmer took the stage for opening remarks, bringing her comedic chops to the program while also speaking about her own experience as a mother. Between jokes, she emphasized the importance of ensuring that all parents have access to the fundamental basics that their children need. She then welcomed the Baby2Baby Angels—Alba, Washington, Ciara, and Teigen—who have supported the organization throughout its growth.

For co-CEOs Kelly Sawyer Patricof and Norah Weinstein, the evening was an opportunity to reflect on just how much has changed since Baby2Baby’s early days. What began as a grassroots operation in Los Angeles has grown into a nationwide organization reaching 1.5 million children annually across all 50 states and distributing more than 850 million items over the past 15 years.

“We started from day one with our mission of providing basic essentials to children in need and knew we had a lot of work ahead of us,” Sawyer Patricof told Vogue. “We didn’t anticipate that our mission would naturally evolve into disaster relief because children living in poverty are the most vulnerable in every situation.”

That expansion has taken Baby2Baby well beyond its original goal of distributing donated diapers and clothing. The organization has now responded to more than 150 disasters nationwide, from the devastating Los Angeles wildfires to hurricanes in Florida and flooding in Texas. Another milestone came through its maternal health initiative. After piloting a program with the Biden administration to provide new mothers with critical supplies immediately after giving birth, Baby2Baby secured a $9 million investment from New York State last year.

The funding, Weinstein told Vogue, will provide Maternal Health & Newborn Supply Kits to 100,000 mothers giving birth on Medicaid across New York, along with millions of diapers for low-income families. “We lit up the Empire State Building in Baby2Baby green to celebrate this milestone,” she added, proudly.

Diaper access, however, remains at the center of Baby2Baby’s work. Over the years, the organization has successfully advocated for the elimination of sales tax on diapers in California and developed its own manufacturing system, allowing it to produce diapers for up to 80% less than average retail cost.

“Diaper need has been at the core of our mission, as nearly half of families in this country are struggling to afford them,” Weinstein and Sawyer Patricof jointly told Vogue. This year brought another major development with California’s first-in-the-nation Golden State Start program, which aims to provide 400 free Baby2Baby diapers to every newborn in the state through participating hospitals.

Back inside the ballroom, the evening’s fundraising efforts were already well underway. By the time Julie Bowen and Jenna Dewan stepped up to kick off the live auction, Baby2Baby had raised an impressive $7.5 million. Guests were encouraged to consider the huge impact of their donations—every $1 million contribution will help to provide 10 million diapers to children in need.

Andrew Garfield soon arrived on the podium to introduce Moore, who was presented with this year’s Giving Tree Award for her advocacy for children and families. A video montage featuring messages from Jon Hamm, Salma Hayek, Drew Barrymore, Jennifer Garner, and Moore’s daughters, among others, paid tribute to the actress before she addressed the room. Moore, whose daughters Rumer, Scout, and Tallulah Willis were in attendance, reflected on motherhood and the importance of having a support system while raising children.

With the most emotive moments behind them, the crowd was ready to celebrate. Katy Perry and Kelly Rowland introduced Cardi B, who sent the crowd wild with a performance of some of her most famous hits. Within moments, there wasn’t a person left sitting down as the ballroom turned into a full-fledged concert.

And the party didn’t stop there. Guests continued on to an after-party where DJ D-Nice kept the music going, while another round of food awaited anyone who had worked up an appetite. Burger She Wrote served late-night burgers, Chainsaw offered sweet treats, and Henry’s Secret Ice Cream and Leora rounded out the dessert offerings.

A 120-Year-Old Gap in Einstein’s Physics May Hold the Key to the Universe’s Earliest Matter

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A missing piece in Einstein’s century-old theory could help scientists understand how matter behaved in the universe’s earliest moments, revealing more about a time we can barely recreate.

In particle accelerators, scientists smashed ions together at nearly the speed of light to recreate the fluid that existed just after the Big Bang. The resulting droplets were roughly the size of an atomic nucleus and survived for only tens of septillionths of a second. One way to investigate their interior was to track heavy quarks, particles that traveled through the droplets without being part of the surrounding fluid.

That fluid, called quark-gluon plasma, offered a glimpse of the universe’s earliest matter. In the first fraction of a second after the Big Bang, matter existed as a trillion-degree mixture of quarks and gluons, subatomic particles moving at close to the speed of light. This state lasted just a few microseconds before the particles came together to form more stable protons and neutrons.

Reading the clues carried by heavy quarks required an accurate description of how the fluid pushed and pulled them. Albert Einstein established a relationship between a particle’s motion and a fluid’s microscopic structure in 1905. At speeds approaching that of light, however, the familiar description missed some of the interactions that shaped a particle’s journey.

Jean Du Plessis, a graduate student in MIT’s Center for Theoretical Physics, a Leinweber Institute, and Bruno Scheihing-Hitschfeld of the Kavli Institute for Theoretical Physics at the University of California at Santa Barbara (UCSB) developed a theory to address that gap. It predicted how a particle moving at relativistic speeds, meaning close to the speed of light, was likely to be pushed and pulled as it passed through a fluid. They reported their findings in Physical Review Letters.

The researchers began with a relativistic heavy quark traveling through a gas of weakly interacting quarks and gluons. Those weak interactions made the calculations manageable. They calculated the forces acting on the heavy quark through the strong nuclear force, the fundamental force that held quarks and gluons together, to determine how its momentum was likely to change.

Their calculation produced a probability distribution, a curve showing how likely different changes in momentum were. It also described the likelihood of kicks of different strengths, since stronger kicks produced larger changes in momentum.

“Our result reveals a new source of information carried by heavy quarks in rare, unusually large changes in their motion,” Du Plessis said. “How often those large changes occur depends on the microscopic nature of the plasma, raising the possibility that they could provide a new way to learn what the quark-gluon plasma is actually like inside.”

The idea of learning about a fluid by watching particles move through it had roots in a much more familiar setting. In 1827, botanist Robert Brown observed grains of pollen jiggling and swirling in seemingly still water. That erratic movement became known as Brownian motion and later inspired Einstein’s work.

Einstein’s 1905 theory explained how invisible activity within a fluid could keep a larger particle moving. Atoms and molecules in the fluid exerted random forces that combined to kick and jostle the particle, while drag from the fluid slowed it down. The relationship between these random kicks and drag determined how the particle eventually reached the same temperature as its surroundings.

Scientists subsequently used Einstein’s explanation to study many fluids, including quark-gluon plasma. They assumed that a particle passing through the plasma would experience a balance of microscopic kicks similar to that experienced by pollen in water. In this familiar picture, the kicks followed a bell-shaped probability distribution. Typical kicks clustered around the peak, while extreme kicks occupied the tails and were equally unlikely in opposite directions.

“Einstein showed that the drag and the random kicks must balance in just the right way for a particle eventually to come to the same temperature as its surroundings,” Du Plessis explained. “For the types of fluids he was thinking about, he was correct. But for particles that are moving at relativistic velocities, this relationship breaks down. And we’ve known for 20 years that something is missing. So we have to go beyond the work that Einstein did.”

The new calculation resembled Einstein’s bell-shaped curve only for small kicks.

“The usual Brownian picture assumes that large kicks become exceedingly rare,” Du Plessis said. “For heavy quarks, however, these rare kicks are more common than the bell curve predicts, and are not equally likely in opposite directions: A large kick that slows the quark down more than expected from drag is more likely than an equally large kick that leaves it moving faster than expected.”

When the researchers repeated their calculations for plasmas made up of different particles, they found the same tilted pattern, with uneven tails at either end of the distribution. They also recognized the same structure in an earlier calculation for a strongly coupled plasma, where particles interacted strongly, despite the differences in the underlying microscopic physics.

“This is the first time that this seemingly universal structure was realized and really formalized,” Du Plessis said. “This gives a concrete path forward for how to better understand the quark-gluon plasma, using heavy quarks.”

The researchers envisioned applying the theory to predict how heavy quarks moved through quark-gluon plasma, then comparing those predictions with experiments. That comparison could help characterize the plasma’s microscopic structure and establish what kind of fluid existed in the universe’s first moments.

Du Plessis said the primordial plasma was likely “more strongly interacting than this Brownian motion way of modeling it would suggest. It’s more like a liquid and less like a gas.”

Reference: “Heavy Quark Transport Is Non-Gaussian beyond Leading Logarithm” by Jean F. Du Plessis and Bruno Scheihing-Hitschfeld, 5 November 2026, Physical Review Letters. DOI: 10.1103/7fd5-1kq5

Research supported in part by the U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Science, Office of Nuclear Physics under grant Contract No. DE-SC0011090, by Grant No. NSF PHY-2309135 to the Kavli Institute for Theoretical Physics (KITP), and by Grant No. 994312 from the Simons Foundation. This research has benefited from the Mitchel Postdoctoral Scholar Career Development Fund at KITP.

Never miss a breakthrough: Join the SciTechDaily newsletter.Follow us on Google and Google News.

A 120-Year-Old Gap in Einstein’s Physics May Hold the Key to the Universe’s Earliest Matter. WHY？

Mainstream physics has developed a highly instrumentalized, terminologized, and project-driven research ecology: (1) Large-scale facilities need to continuously generate news, and therefore tend to adopt expressions such as “unlocking,” “hidden,” “strange,” and “quantum breakthrough”; (2) Peer review tends to reward publishable labels for new phenomena rather than deeper geometric unification; (3) “Quantum materials” have gradually become a tool for funding allocation and journal classification, with their conceptual boundaries being continually stretched; (4) Experimental precision keeps rising, yet much work remains at the level of phenomenological classification, lacking a fundamental reconstruction of phase fields, vortex topology, and fluid geometry. This has become a brazenly shameless state of affairs in mainstream physics.

Einstein’s sorrowful silhouette has faded into the distance. But his intuition, his persistence, his belief in “local realism”, has finally found its rightful home in the framework of Topological Vortex Theory (TVT). Einstein was not a failed prophet, but a true physicist misunderstood by his era.

Is Meta Stock Still a Buy After Borrowing About $25 Billion This Month?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The social media giant's new bonds pay up to 7.5% a year. Should shareholders worry?

Meta Platforms (META +2.90%) has been raising money quickly. In late October, it priced a stock sale at $625 a share along with a sale of mandatory convertible preferred stock. Next, the social media company sold $15 billion in dollar bonds on Nov. 3 and €9 billion in euro bonds (around $10 billion) on Nov. 4. The euro deal was its first.

Meta borrowed about $25 billion this month. And the stock trades near $640 as I write, about 18% below its Sept. 24 closing high of about $778.

Is Meta stock still a buy with the extra debt? I think so, but the borrowing comes right before what might be Meta's most expensive quarter yet.

Meta said it plans to use the proceeds for general corporate purposes. Much of it will probably go toward the servers, data centers, and network infrastructure behind its capital spending.

Showing how quickly that spending has risen, Meta's capital expenditures (including principal payments on finance leases) climbed from $19.8 billion in the first three months of 2026 to $31.1 billion in the second quarter and $34.9 billion in the third quarter. Operating cash flow, meanwhile, stayed at around $32 billion to $33 billion every quarter. So, free cash flow (the cash left after capital spending) dropped from $12.4 billion in Q1 to $784 million in Q2 and then to negative $2.1 billion in Q3.

And the biggest spending quarter is probably still ahead. Management expects 2026 capital expenditures of $135 billion to $145 billion. With around $85.8 billion spent through September, the guidance points to another $49 billion to $59 billion by year-end.

If fourth-quarter operating cash flow comes in near the third quarter's $32.8 billion, Meta's spending could top it by about $16 billion to $26 billion. The new bonds raised around $25 billion after fees, plus about $14.3 billion from the preferred stock, which pays a 6.5% annual dividend.

Together, these amounts exceed a quarter's shortfall, and more is coming. Meta won't get the roughly $28.7 billion from its common stock sale until forward sale agreements settle, by Nov. 19, 2027, at the latest. Of course, management has said it plans to keep "investing aggressively" next year, too.

Meta owed $59 billion in notes at the end of 2025, and that face value rose to $84 billion by Sept. 30 after a $25 billion bond deal in May. This month's bonds bring it to around $109 billion.

Borrowing has gotten pricier, too. The new dollar notes pay interest rates of 5.85% (due 2031) to 7.5% (due 2056). Even the lowest beats the highest rate on any Meta bond sold before 2026, which was 5.75%.

The euro notes came in lower, at 4.3% to 6.4%, partly because benchmark interest rates in Europe sit below those in the U.S.

On Meta's third-quarter earnings call, chief financial officer Susan Li said the new Class C stock was part of a push "to ensure that we have access to a diverse pool of funding sources."

In the third quarter, Meta's interest expense more than quadrupled year over year to $977 million, and the new bonds add about $1.5 billion annually.

I think Meta can cover that bill. After all, it generated $22.9 billion in operating income in the third quarter alone, not counting a $9.89 billion legal charge. And Moody's still rates the debt Aa3 with a stable outlook, but S&P Global's AA- rating has a negative one.

Meta's ad revenue, which pays for all this, climbed 26% year over year to $63.1 billion in the third quarter. Total revenue growth has slowed, from 33% in Q1 to 28% and then 26%. But management expects fourth-quarter revenue between $73 billion and $76 billion, indicating growth of about 22% to 27%.

And the stock doesn't look expensive. At roughly $640, Meta trades at about 18 times the earnings expected for 2027, near the 17.7 times forward earnings FactSet put on the S&P 500 on Nov. 5. So investors are paying about a market-average price for a company growing revenue over 20%.

Spending could outrun cash for longer than investors think, of course, and more borrowing at 6% to 7.5% could follow.

In the end, I think the extra debt is a fair cost for a business growing so fast, and the shares look worth buying around $640. I'd just want to see free cash flow go positive again once this round of spending peaks.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: PSA: &quot;Lock Credentials&quot; seems a new setting at Vanguard?

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →


My assumption is that today, there are two options for a password reset:

1. online reset facility
2. calling Vanguard

and that the credential lock eliminates the 1st one.

Many Vanguard users have FIDO2 2FA implemented with a pair (or more) of Yubikeys, and SMS 2FA turned off. A password reset won’t provide won’t provide access in that scenario anyway. One instead could create passkeys deployed to the Yubikeys and set the credential lock, putting someone in the same boat.

My guess is that implementing the credential lock instead of dropping having passwords is so that when someone calls in due to being locked out, a rep and just turn off the credential lock to restore their access.
Is that then one of the weak links - calling Vanguard and overriding the various security measures?
Social engineering attacks are always in play with any service.

Nebraska MBB Recap: Huskers Turn the Broncos to Glue 102-75

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This one was expected to be an offensive onslaught, and it seemed to be heading that way. Nebraska started the game 5-6 from the floor, but Boise State was 3-5 from deep. A little bit of sloppiness ensued, as both teams would commit multiple turnovers in the opening 5 minutes. The Huskers would use balanced scoring, as Damon Wilkinson, Braden Frager and Cale Jacobsen each had 7 points in the opening 10 minutes.

Boise would keep themselves in the contest with decent shooting from beyond the arc. The Broncos finally hit their first shot inside the arc at the 11:28 mark. Nebraska would open up the lead to double digits before Boise State immediately countered with an 8-0 run. Nebraska answered with 6 straight and didn't look back. The Huskers would eventually end the half on an 8-0 run themselves and took a 52-34 lead into the locker room.

Boise State went 12-31 for 39% from the fl0or. They went 8-19 for 42% from deep and 2-5 for 40% from the free-throw line. The Broncos collected only 7 rebounds, and zero offensive. They had 11 assists and 9 turnovers.

The Broncos were led by Demari Wheeler-Thomas, who had 9 points, 2 rebounds and 5 assists. Spencer Ahrens had 2 rebounds to tie Wheeler-Thomas and Aginaldo Neto contributed 3 assists.

Nebraska went 20-30 for 67% from the floor. They went 3-8 for 38% from deep and 9-10 for 90% from the free-throw line. The Huskers collected 19 rebounds, only one of which was offensive. Nebraska had 11 assists to 10 turnovers. Nebraska had 32 points in the paint.

The Huskers were led by Braden Frager, who had 16 points. Cale Jacobsen had 12 points and 5 rebounds. Taj DeGourville had 4 assists.

Nebraska has sometimes not come out of the locker room shooting the ball well after a hot shooting first half. That problem was dispelled quickly. Frager quickly became the first one to 20+ points on the contest. Huskers would have a little bit of foul trouble, as Frager and Jacobsen would pick up their 3rd fouls in the opening 3 minutes. Boise State would still be shooting the ball relatively well from beyond the arc, but they eventually went a little cold.

Nebraska absolutely blew the door open, as the Huskers would shoot over 60% for the entire contest. Frager and Jacobsen would eventually both foul out for Nebraska, but by then it didn't matter. This game was in control the whole half. Nebraska's biggest lead hit 33 points, with 5 different Huskers eventually finishing with double figures.

Boise State went 25-66 for 38% from the floor. They went 13-35 for 37% from deep and 12-17 for 71% from the free-throw line. The Broncos collected 24 rebounds, 8 of which were offensive. Boise State had 19 assists to 15 turnovers.

The Broncos were led by Aginaldo Neto and Spencer Ahrens, who had 17 points each. Dovydas Butka had 7 rebounds. Demari Wheeler-Thomas had 8 assists.

Nebraska went 38-57 for 67% from the floor. They went 12-24 for 50% from deep and 14-18 for 78% from the free-throw line. The Huskers collected 35 rebounds, only 3 of which were offensive. Nebraska had 26 assists to 17 turnovers. They had 50 points in the paint and 33 bench points.

The Huskers were led by Braden Frager, who had a career high 26 points. Frager also led the way with 7 rebounds. Taj DeGourville led the way with 8 assists. Pryce Sandfort had 3 steals.

This was a good day for Nebraska. They got plenty of good looks from everywhere on the floor. Boise State has a good offense, but the Husker defense did a good job of limiting the Broncos from beyond the arc in the second half. This was a decent litmus test and the Huskers passed with flying colors. Despite some sloppiness for a few minutes here and there, particularly by the reserves, Nebraska dominated this from start to finish. Boise State never got within single digits after 3 minutes left in the first half.

The Huskers return home to play South Dakota. Tip-off from Pinnacle Bank Arena on Wednesday night will be at 7:30 pm and can be seen on the Big Ten Network.

Netflix Officially Confirms Its Next Major Crime Thriller Is Coming in 2027

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

2026 has been a great year for Netflix's crime thrillers. Some of the platform's most popular shows and movies have been in this genre, cementing Netflix as a great destination for fans seeking high-octane stories. From I Will Find You to Nemesis and The Rip, there have been many stories to enjoy. These releases have dominated global watch charts, sparking widespread fan theories and watercooler debates. I Will Find You remains Netflix's most popular show of 2026, underscoring Harlan Coben's knack for crafting twisty, engaging crime thrillers.

Ultimately, 2026 hasn't just delivered quantity for thriller enthusiasts — it has raised the bar for streaming suspense, ensuring audiences remain on the edge of their seats all year long. But with the year drawing to a close, it is time to look ahead to what the streamer has on the horizon, and 2027 starts in style with another crime thriller, The Twin, that features powerful performers in the lead roles.

Jamie Dornan (Fifty Shades of Grey) plays dual roles as Adam and Lee in the six-part British crime noir series adapted from the acclaimed Norwegian series, Twin. Mackenzie Davis (Station Eleven) also stars as Nicola. The new adaptation trades Norway's rugged archipelago of the Lofoten Islands for the moody, isolated backdrop of the Scottish Highlands. Below, the show's official logline teases the series' events.

"Suffocating in a loveless marriage to Adam, Nicola's life takes a dramatic turn when Adam's long-estranged identical twin brother Lee comes crashing back into her life, and their tangled romantic past threatens to destroy the present. A split-second decision and a terrible accident drive Nicola to protect her children, and over the course of one week, Lee and Nicola are forced together, struggling to maintain a web of secrets and lies. Though they can't ignore their feelings for each other, they both know they're living on borrowed time."

Britain makes more detective television than any country has any business making, and somehow almost all of it is good. Here is the one you belong in, what it says about you, and the case for it being the best of the four.

You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

The Twin is written by Sarah Dollard (Neighbors), Hanna Jameson, Scout Cripps, Kam Odedra (Lupin), and Joy Wilkinson (Lockwood & Co.), and is directed by Jeremy Lovering. The series — previously titled The Undertow — is based on the Norwegian series Twin, created by Kristoffer Metcalfe. The original series has a 7.1/10 score on IMDb, indicating mixed reviews from the 2000 users who rated it. Other cast members include Iain De Caestecker (The Winter King) as Marty and Gary Lewis (Billy Elliot) as Keith.

The Twin hits Netflix on Friday, January 1, 2027. Stay tuned to Collider for more updates.

2 Brilliant Stocks to Buy Before 2027

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

These two home-related businesses are still growing in a weak market.

Few areas of the economy look much worse than housing right now. Existing-home sales dropped 8.8% year over year in October, according to the National Association of Realtors, as mortgage rates reached their highest levels since 2000, pushing home sales to their lowest level since August 2010.

You might expect every business tied to the home to be struggling. Yet a couple of leading retail and paint brands are growing right through the slump, making these stocks compelling buys if housing begins to stabilize next year.

Shares of Wayfair (W +0.40%), the online home furnishings retailer, have nearly doubled since its May 19 close and are up 11% this year through Nov. 12. Weak demand for home goods sent the stock down over the past five years, but the business has been gaining strength.

Third-quarter revenue rose 9.7% year over year to $3.4 billion, with U.S. revenue up 11.4%. Operating income nearly tripled to $106 million.

The improvement in profitability stands out. "Over the past two years, we have grown orders faster while spending less on advertising as a share of revenue," CEO Niraj Shah said on the earnings call.

Orders delivered grew 12.2% in the quarter, while ad dollars rose just 2.4%, pushing advertising down to 9.9% of revenue. Extra holiday marketing will push it back to around 11% of revenue in the fourth quarter. Still, last quarter's single-digit result shows that Wayfair is building a brand that attracts customers with minimal marketing.

Another positive is market share gains. Wayfair's U.S. sales are growing faster than the home furnishings market as a whole, a sign it's taking customers from competitors. Newer initiatives -- Wayfair Rewards, Wayfair Verified, its Delivery+ program, and physical stores -- are still early in contributing to results, and management believes its share gains can continue to build into 2027 and beyond. When the industry returns to growth, those share gains could contribute meaningfully to revenue growth.

There are still headwinds for Wayfair. International revenue fell 2.3% year over year, and a longer housing slump would likely weigh on its domestic sales. But at about 27 times 2027 adjusted earnings, its valuation looks reasonable, given that adjusted earnings per share climbed 24% year over year in the first nine months of 2026 and management aims to keep growing adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) faster than revenue.

Shares of Sherwin-Williams (SHW -0.20%), a leading paint and coatings maker, are up just 3% over the past year through Nov. 12. That modest gain overlooks a business that continues to grow in a weak market.

Consolidated sales growth has accelerated for five straight quarters, from 3.2% in the third quarter of 2025 to 7.9% in the latest quarter, when sales reached nearly $6.9 billion. Part of that growth came from Suvinil, the Brazilian paint business it acquired last year. Still, management says it is gaining share across its end markets.

Profitability is holding up. Gross margin slipped to 47.8% from 49.2% due to higher raw material costs and the Suvinil acquisition, but the adjusted EBITDA margin of 20.5% remained within the company's targeted range. The business should also benefit from an 8% price increase, which is expected to begin showing up in sales in the first half of 2027.

There are more catalysts in 2027 that don't include a housing recovery, such as $75 million in expected cost savings and a return to 80-100 net new store openings. None of that depends on housing. "We are not building that on a macro recovery that we do not control," CFO Ben Meisenzahl said of the company's guidance.

The main risk is that higher raw material costs outpace price increases, which could drag the stock down. Shares trade at about 27 times 2027 consensus estimates, with analysts expecting roughly 11% annual earnings growth in the years ahead.

That valuation looks expensive, but investors have historically paid a high earnings multiple for Sherwin-Williams, which has a long history of growth. It's a relatively safe stock to hold through a housing slump and profit when the market eventually rebounds.

Honda Did Make Diesel Engines, Here's Why They Disappeared

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We all know and love Honda for its high-revving gasoline engines (remember the S600's 9,500-rpm motor?) and, more recently, its frugal hybrid powertrains. However, the Japanese automaker's legacy isn't just built on gasoline, as it used to offer a family of diesel engines that powered popular cars like the Civic, Accord, CR-V, and City.

Interestingly, Honda's former senior chief engineer, Kenichi Nagahiro, was far from a fan of diesel engines due to their noise and smell. But diesel was booming in Europe in the early 2000s, so Nagahiro locked in and built Honda's first in-house diesel engine — the 2.2-liter i-CTDi. And since Nagahiro hated diesel engines in their then-current forms, he ensured the 2.2-liter i-CTDi would live up to his standards.

Honda also introduced diesel engines for the Indian market back in 2013, giving buyers a more fuel-efficient alternative in models such as the Amaze and City. However, that changed over the following decade as emissions regulations became stricter and customer preferences shifted toward gasoline and hybrid vehicles. Honda began phasing out diesel models in Europe in 2019, with the withdrawal completed by 2021. In India, Honda continued selling diesel cars for longer but ended production in early 2023. By then, India was the last place Honda still built diesel engines, including those for CR-Vs sold in Thailand and the Philippines. These decisions were not down to any single problem, and certainly not reliability (after all, diesel engines generally last longer than their gas counterparts). Instead, they were a consequence of the growing cost of keeping these engines compliant with tightening emissions regulations, declining demand, and Honda's changing powertrain strategy.

The 2.2-liter four-cylinder i-CTDi debuted in 2003 and appeared in European-market versions of the Accord and, later, the Civic, CR-V, and FR-V. The 2.2-liter i-CTDi was compliant with Euro 4 emissions regulations, had a rated mileage of 43.5 mpg on Europe's official test cycle (52.3 mpg in imperial gallons), and produced 138 hp and 251 lb-ft of torque. In 2007, Honda introduced an updated Euro-5-compliant version of the engine, dubbed the 2.2-liter i-DTEC, which also powered the Accord, Civic, and CR-V in several markets. The engine developed 147 to 178 hp and 258 to 280 lb-ft of torque.

In 2012, Honda introduced its smaller 1.6-liter four-cylinder i-DTEC, part of its "Earth Dreams" line of engines. The 1.6-liter i-DTEC was over 100 pounds lighter and more fuel-efficient (65.4 mpg in the Civic; 78.5 in imperial gallons) than the 2.2-liter i-DTEC, and attracted lower taxes in Europe because it dipped below the 100-gram-per-kilometer mark for carbon dioxide emissions. In Europe, the 1.6-liter i-DTEC found its way under the hoods of the Civic, CR-V, and HR-V. It was also used in the Civic and CR-V sold in India, with the latter also available in Thailand and the Philippines. Output ranged from 118 to 158 hp and 221 to 258 lb-ft of torque.

Then came the 1.5-liter four-cylinder i-DTEC in 2013 — Honda's final in-house diesel engine. Developed specifically for India, the 1.5-liter i-DTEC was essentially a downsized variant of the 1.6-liter i-DTEC engine, and made its debut in the first-generation Amaze. Honda subsequently offered the 1.5-liter i-DTEC in the India-spec City, Mobilio, Jazz, BR-V, and WR-V. The engine developed 100 hp and 148 lb-ft of torque, and was rated for staggeringly good fuel efficiency of up to 64.4 mpg on India's official test cycle (27.4 km/l).

The main reason Honda abandoned diesel cars was the increasingly difficult economics of keeping them compliant with tougher emissions regulations, especially in light of decreasing global demand. In Europe, the aftermath of Volkswagen's Dieselgate emissions scandal invited greater scrutiny of diesel cars, while stricter Euro 6 requirements and real-world emissions testing increased the engineering burden on automakers. Compared to gasoline engines, diesels generally require more elaborate exhaust after-treatment systems to control emissions. Updating these systems, validating them, and certifying them for different models adds development and production costs. Honda announced in 2019 that it would phase out diesel cars in Europe by 2021 as it shifted toward electrified vehicles.

India presented a similar challenge. The introduction of Bharat Stage 6 (BS6) emissions standards in April 2020 had already made diesel engines more expensive to update, and the implementation of real-driving emissions requirements in April 2023 added further complications. Honda decided that the investment needed to upgrade its 1.5-liter i-DTEC engine was not justified by its sales figures.

According to Autocar Professional, diesel accounted for just 7% of Honda India's sales, down from 21% after the BS6 transition in 2020. Honda also told Autocar Professional that gasoline cars accounted for more than 90% of its overall sales in India. The report noted that the wider market was moving the same way: in the decade leading up to 2022, diesel's share of India's passenger vehicle market shrank to 20%, down from a peak of 54%. Honda India ceased production of diesel engines by early 2023, including the 1.6-liter i-DTEC mill it was exporting to Thailand. And so, in a span of just two decades, Honda closed the book on diesel engines for good.

Why Investors Should Own Businesses That Can Win Under Any Administration

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The United States holds elections every two years, which is too short a period to justify a long-term investment.

It is easy to get excited about big news events, and few things have been bigger lately than politics. Regardless of your political views, tying your investment strategy to a Republican or Democratic success story isn't likely to set you up for long-term investment success. Why? Because the United States holds elections every two years.

In other words, the political winds can change very quickly. And they often do, noting that the big Democratic win in the midterm elections came just two years after a big Republican win during the presidential election. This is why you may want to consider buying companies like Procter & Gamble (PG +1.05%), Federal Realty (FRT +0.35%), and NextEra Energy (NEE -0.20%). Here's a deeper dive into each one and the sector they represent.

Procter & Gamble is one of the world's largest consumer staples companies. It is a Dividend King, with over 50 annual dividend increases. It offers an attractive and well-above-market 2.6% dividend yield. But the real story here is that it sells products you are going to buy regardless of who occupies the White House or controls the Senate and House. In fact, you'll keep buying the toilet paper, toothpaste, and cough medicines that P&G makes right through bear markets and recessions, too. These products are life necessities.

What makes P&G extra attractive is that it has long focused on leading the industry through innovation. The goal is to make products that customers are willing to pay for, which was on clear display in the first quarter of fiscal 2027. Despite budget tightening among consumers, P&G organic sales rose a healthy 4%. P&G is a very well-run consumer staples business, and even the most conservative investor should feel comfortable owning it for the long term. But the business is a strong representative of a sector whose demand isn't tied to the political cycle.

Fedearl Realty is the only Dividend King real estate investment trust (REIT). It uses a sharpshooter approach, owning only about 100 strip-mall and mixed-use properties. Essentially, its properties are located in areas with large populations of wealthy customers. It also invests significant time and money in development and redevelopment to ensure its properties are leading destinations in the regions they serve. Retailers want to be located in Fedeal Realty assets because the locations can't be replicated.

The stock's yield is hovering around 4% right now, but the real story here is all about location, location, location. A REIT with a great portfolio of assets isn't watching the political cycle. It focuses on serving its tenants and their customers well because the properties it owns will remain for decades. Two years is a drop in the bucket. Federal Realty's dividend record sets it apart from its peers, but it represents an industry that can't make decisions on a two-year schedule. In fact, it can take that long to just build an asset.

NextEra Energy is one of the world's largest utilities. It has increased its dividend annually for 31 years. (Black Hills (BKH -0.95%) is a Dividend King utility, for those that want to stick to stocks with the longest dividend records.) NextEra operates regulated utility assets and owns a large portfolio of unregulated renewable power assets. The dividend yield is an attractive 2.9%.

On the surface, NextEra is tightly tied to politics because of its government-regulated operations. But there's a catch: the modern world couldn't operate without electricity. So NextEra has to learn to work with whatever political group is running the show, but every political group has a vested interest in having a positive working relationship with NextEra, too. That's the norm in the utility sector, even though tensions can arise at times. What's especially attractive about NextEra today is that it has two avenues for growth, operating in both regulated and unregulated spaces. Consumers and government leaders want it to succeed on both sides of its business.

It's easy to get caught up in the emotions of a moment, and politics can elicit very strong emotions. But you have to keep in mind that the political cycle in the United States is very short, which is why you should try to keep your investment plans separate from your political expectations. You want to own businesses, like P&G in the consumer staples sector, Federal Realty in the REIT niche, and NextEra Energy in the utility space, that have businesses that can succeed regardless of which party is currently in favor.

Sources: The real reason Crystal Palace want to appoint Thomas Frank revealed

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Transfer Correspondent AUTHORITY Former Sky Sports and ESPN FC correspondent who specialises in global recruitment, contract negotiations, and high-velocity transfer breaking news. FOCUS Exclusive reporting, domestic and international transfer markets, and elite-level insights. THE INSIGHT Pete utilises a Global Network of Industry Sources to deliver verified, first-to-market reporting. His “Deep-Lens” analysis connects the dots between boardroom strategy and pitch-level recruitment, providing a level of transfer clarity that goes beyond surface-level rumours.

Crystal Palace are closing in on an agreement for Thomas Frank to become their next manager, sources have told Football Insider.

Pierre Sage's dismissal was not in the plans for the South London club, who had been keen to stick with the Frenchman, but they have now been forced into a change.

The former Tottenham and Brentford boss has quickly emerged as the front-runner for the position at Selhurst Park, with talks having taken place throughout this week.

As I revealed on the latest edition of Football Insider's Transfer Insider podcast, Frank's Premier League experience and remarkable work with Brentford have made him the top choice.

The boss is also viewed as a good motivator, which is something Palace are searching for to improve their morale having dropped to the bottom of the Premier League table.

Crystal Palace hope Frank's reign will reflect the work he did with Brentford rather than with Tottenham, where he struggled, and believe he is the right man for the job.

Football Insider revealed on 13 November that Crystal Palace are locked in talks with Frank to become their next manager and are making progress in their discussions.

A number of other candidates have been considered, including the likes of Sean Dyche and Vitor Pereira, but the former Brentford boss has always been the first choice.

Palace had initially hoped to have his appointment wrapped up quickly, but despite the drawn-out timeframe, they still believe he will be the man to deliver positive results.

Speaking on the Transfer Insider podcast, I explained: "Discussions are happening between Crystal Palace and Thomas Frank, they have made him their number one target to replace Pierre Sage.

"Obviously the key thing for Thomas Frank is that he's got the Premier League experience that Palace probably need right now to get them out of their current predicament.

"They want to make sure they're not involved in a relegation battle as the season goes on, and think Frank is the man to get them out of that.

"His CV is quite good. He's a good motivator as well. He did a great job at Brentford and I'm sure Palace will be hoping that he could repeat his success that he had at Brentford at Crystal Palace.

"The squad is good at Palace as well, I think it's just about getting the best out of them, and I'm sure that Palace feel that Thomas Frank is the right man to turn things around at Selhurst Park."

As previously mentioned, Crystal Palace had hoped to have the appointment of their next manager wrapped up closely after Sage's exit earlier this week.

However, one week after the 3-1 defeat against Liverpool which put the writing on the wall for Sage, they have still not reached an agreement with their top target.

Football Insider revealed on 13 November that Frank's appointment has hit a new stumbling block regarding the backroom team who would join him at Selhurst Park.

The Eagles are currently working to negotiate deals with Frank's rather sizable team, as they aim to back him with his trusted allies after doing the same for Sage previously.

While the deal is not expected to face any major issues as a result, the ongoing negotiations explain the reason for the delays surrounding Frank's official appointment.

China Completes First Piloted Flight Of 19-Seat Electric Plane Built To Fly Between Cities

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

China has completed the first piloted flight of the CE-25A, a 19-seat all-electric aircraft developed for short-distance passenger services between cities. Built by the Commercial Aircraft Corporation of China (COMAC) with domestic partners, the aircraft flew on November 14 in Binzhou, Shandong Province, carrying two pilots and a flight-test engineer. The milestone moves the project beyond its earlier autonomous testing, although commercial operations will depend on completing China’s aircraft certification process.

The CE-25A is designed for a maximum range of 215 nautical miles (400 km), a cruising speed of 186 miles per hour (300 km/h), and a maximum takeoff weight of 22,046 pounds (10,000 kg). Its electric propulsion system uses multiple ducted fans distributed across the wings, rather than conventional turboprop engines. COMAC says the aircraft performed normally during the piloted flight, but the test does not yet demonstrate that it can carry paying passengers over its advertised range under airline operating conditions.

The CE-25A uses a distributed electric propulsion system with ten ducted fans mounted above its wings, five on each side. Electric motors drive the fans, replacing the conventional turboprop engines typically found on aircraft serving similar regional markets. This arrangement is intended to provide propulsion while reducing reliance on fossil fuels, although COMAC has not publicly disclosed the aircraft's battery capacity, energy consumption, or charging requirements.

Development involved a partnership between COMAC, battery manufacturer Contemporary Amperex Technology Co. Limited (CATL), Shanghai Jiao Tong University, and other domestic organizations. CATL's involvement is particularly relevant because battery energy density remains one of the central constraints on electric aviation. Batteries store substantially less energy per unit of weight than aviation fuel, making it difficult for all-electric aircraft to match the range and payload of conventional designs.

The CE-25A's 10-tonne maximum takeoff weight reflects the challenge of carrying enough battery capacity while retaining space for passengers and other equipment. COMAC has not released a detailed cabin layout or confirmed how much payload the aircraft can carry while achieving its stated range. Those details will be necessary to assess its commercial usefulness.

The November flight follows a series of autonomous tests that began on February 2, 2026, reported by AeroTime. COMAC says the aircraft has completed 19 unmanned flight validations, allowing engineers to assess its systems and performance before placing pilots aboard. The latest milestone adds another stage to the development program, but it does not mean the aircraft is ready to enter airline service.

China's Civil Aviation Administration has accepted COMAC's application for a type certificate. This begins the formal process of demonstrating that the aircraft meets applicable airworthiness requirements. Certification will involve regulatory scrutiny of its design, systems, performance, and operating safety, alongside testing to establish that the aircraft can perform reliably under the conditions expected in service.

The CE-25A designation also reflects its intended regulatory category. The number 25 refers to China's CCAR-25 transport-category airworthiness standards, which apply to larger commercial aircraft. Meeting those requirements is a substantial undertaking for a new electric aircraft, particularly given the different safety considerations associated with high-voltage electrical systems, battery thermal management, and distributed propulsion. COMAC has not announced a certification completion date, launch customer, aircraft price, or planned entry into commercial service. The company will need to demonstrate not only that the aircraft can fly, but that it can operate safely and consistently within the demands of a commercial schedule.

This compact variant is designed for routes that serve airports on high plateaus.

The CE-25A's published 400-km range places it in the short-distance regional market rather than the broader commuter segment served by conventional turboprops. Its intended role is connecting cities over relatively short distances, potentially providing an alternative where road or rail journeys are less convenient. It's cruising speed, however, is slower than several existing aircraft in the same 19-seat category.

For comparison, the Dornier 228, De Havilland Canada Twin Otter, and Cessna SkyCourier can serve similar passenger markets while offering substantially greater range under their respective published performance assumptions. Their figures are not directly comparable because manufacturers use different payload, fuel, and reserve conditions. Nevertheless, the comparison illustrates the operating limitations an electric aircraft must overcome before competing across a wider network. The CE-25A's stated range also should not be interpreted as the distance it can routinely fly with a full passenger load. Airlines must account for fuel or energy reserves, weather, diversions, and operational margins. Charging infrastructure and turnaround times will also influence how frequently the aircraft can operate.

For now, the most realistic opportunity is likely to involve short domestic routes where the aircraft's range is sufficient and charging facilities can be established at both ends. Its first piloted flight demonstrates progress toward that possibility, but certification, payload capability, and operating economics will determine whether the CE-25A can become a viable commercial aircraft.
