London City's 'bravery' paying off in top three pursuit

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Izzy Goodwin scored from her one shot on target against Arsenal

After 10 games in the Women's Super League season, London City Lionesses have shown they are capable of finishing inside the top three.

Their narrow 2-1 defeat by Manchester City two weeks ago provided snippets of their potential but a 1-1 draw at the Emirates against an improved Arsenal on Sunday confirmed their credentials.

Eder Maestre's side sit third in the table - the last qualification spot for next season's Women's Champions League - and are six points above Arsenal.

It is a strong position to be in just shy of the halfway stage having already faced big hitters Arsenal, Manchester City and Manchester United.

Current leaders Chelsea await in December but, for now, London City's progress is moving at a pace that looks set to challenge the usual suspects.

Find interviews and extra content on the BBC Women's Football Weekly feed

Goodwin's glorious solo strike draws London City Lionesses level

A sold-out away end helped fill out the 32,000-strong crowd at the Emirates on Sunday as many hoped for an end-to-end encounter.

They were not disappointed as fearless London City showed bravery against an Arsenal side who played with a spark following their 1-1 draw with Barcelona.

The visitors were helped out by Arsenal's wastefulness - the Gunners had 17 shots and only converted one - but London City caused plenty of problems of their own.

Maestre said after their defeat by Manchester City that their opponents showed more experience and know-how but against Arsenal, his side matured.

They were under pressure early on and fell a goal down thanks to Frida Maanum's opener but it took just 98 seconds for Izzy Goodwin to equalise for London City.

That killed Arsenal's momentum and when the pressure increased again from the hosts in the second half, goalkeeper and player of the match Mary Earps stepped up to make crucial saves.

"The performance was there. We had the chances to score and get a better result but Arsenal had chances too. The game was very open," said Maestre.

"The way we faced and challenged every circumstance in the game shows we are here, and able to play with that mentality in these big games.

"We came here to take risks. Our energy was good. We were brave and weren't scared about the context or the opponent.

"We tried to play as if we were at home. I always want to feel that my teams are brave and go for the win."

That bravery showed as Alexia Putellas came close twice with long-range efforts, Goodwin could have been onside when she was played in behind later on.

London City hit the crossbar through substitute Delphine Cascarino and had 14 shots compared to Arsenal's 17, including four inside the box.

Arsenal are still yet to win at home this season in the WSL but this was still an impressive showing from London City, who came to the Emirates to play.

But while they were willing to take risks, they were smarter than they were against Manchester City, choosing when to press aggressively and when to hold off.

"We tried to match the pace of the ball and not expose ourselves as much to the players that Arsenal have," added Maestre.

"We were trying to mix the moments we were more clinical in our press. We made some adjustments at half-time and were much better at recognising those."

Mary Earps made eight saves in the 1-1 draw with Arsenal

London City are backed by US billionaire Michele Kang and that has made a big difference in the transfer market.

They recruited superstars Alexia Putellas, Mary Earps, Mapi Leon and Kadidiatou Diani in the summer.

Such is their depth that high-flying Spaniard Lucia Corrales, the dangerous Cascarino and England international Freya Godfrey came off the bench.

Those individual talents have made a difference and on Sunday it was goalkeeper Earps who took the plaudits, claiming the player of the match award.

She made crucial saves late on as Arsenal pushed ferociously for a winner, tipping Maanum's effort onto the crossbar and blocking her shot from close range.

Earps had to bide her time for the number one shirt after making the switch from Paris St-Germain this summer but has started the past three WSL games.

"Mary is a very strong character and is much more than the things we see in terms of the performance," said Maestre.

"She has experience, and energy that you can bring to every part in the process and not just the things we see in the matches.

"I think the energy that she brings to the position is massive and has a massive impact. I'm very pleased with her."

Watching on in the stands was England boss Sarina Wiegman who will have been pleased with the display from goalscorer Goodwin.

The 23-year-old got the better of England captain Leah Williamson when she fooled her with quick feet and fired the ball into the back of the net, making it 1-1.

She is proof that competition for places in the London City starting XI is pushing players on and that their depth is improving each week.

"I think Izzy is becoming a more mature player. She has the inner competition and the signings sometimes push you to bring 100% every time," added Maestre.

"She is committed to keep pushing and, with the performances she is having, why not [earn an England call-up]?

"Izzy is moving forward in the ways she can take advantage and that commitment is the way she is reaching those performances. I'm very proud of her."

Delta Air Lines Launches New 9-Hour Nonstop Route: See Map Now

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Delta Air Lines began operations on a new seasonal route from Minneapolis St. Paul International Airport (MSP) in Minnesota to Kahului Airport (OGG) on Maui. Having not previously been served, not even by Northwest Airlines before it was merged into Delta, Maui was a key unserved market from MSP, with 66,000 passengers flying each year indirectly.

While a domestic route, the length of these flights, which are blocked at almost nine hours in the westbound direction, means that long-haul comfort is required. Delta provides this with the Airbus A330-300, which it operates with 282 seats in a three-class configuration. This new route is seasonal, running until mid-April, and, towards the end of its tenure, coverage will switch from the A330 to the Boeing 767.

Delta Air Lines operated its first flight from Minneapolis to Maui on Monday. According to a corresponding statement released at the time by the Metropolitan Airports Commission (MAC), the Atlanta-based US 'big three' legacy carrier and SkyTeam founding member plans to serve this route five times a week. However, during peak holiday periods and in the spring break, extra frequencies will be added.

Delta first announced its plans to link Minneapolis with Maui back in February, giving nine months for excitement around the route to build. On this front, Amy Martin, Vice President of Network Planning at Delta, said the route "strengthens connectivity from an important US hub while giving Upper Midwest customers more choice."

Brian Ryks, the CEO of the MAC, spoke about the "demand" between MSP and OGG.

"Demand for Maui is high: it’s MSP’s largest unserved nonstop destination. This route will give more options for travelers seeking warm and sunny vacation options over the winter and early spring seasons."

At close to nine hours, the westbound leg of this route is one of the longest domestic services in Delta Air Lines' network. Numbered as DL726, it is scheduled to depart from Minneapolis at 11:53 AM local time. This makes it a handy option for passengers arriving in Minneapolis on early-morning flights. It is scheduled to arrive in Maui at 4:29 PM local time, about eight hours and 36 minutes later.

Of course, with block time including taxi time at both ends, the actual time spent in the air is closer to the eight-hour mark. Data from Flightradar24 shows that, on November 9, the inaugural flight took eight hours and six minutes, departing at 12:02 PM and landing at 4:08 PM. The return leg is flight DL805 and operates overnight, offering ideal early-morning connectivity upon arrival.

Found an error? Send it info@simpleflying.com so it can be corrected.

After a planned 90-minute turnaround on the ground in Maui, this service is scheduled to depart at 5:59 PM local time, arriving back in Minneapolis at 5:34 AM the next day. This gives it a block time of even hours and 35 minutes. The inaugural iteration of this leg on Monday spent seven hours and nine minutes in the air, taking off at 6:13 PM local time and touching down at 5:22 AM.

Delta unveils its biggest Delta One overhaul in years, featuring luxury dining, enhanced comfort and global expansion in 2027.

According to Cirium, an aviation analytics company, Delta plans to operate a grand total of 100 rotations on this route with the Airbus A330-300, lasting until late March. However, with the corridor continuing to be served until mid-April, it has thereafter penciled in a further 15 round trips between MSP and Maui with the Boeing 767-300ER. A similar switch is planned for its other MSP-Hawaii route.

This corridor links Minneapolis with Daniel K. Inouye International Airport (HNL) in Honolulu. Delta serves this route daily with the A330-300 over the winter, but in mid-April, it will also switch to the 767-300ER. This will correspond to a reduction in frequency to four flights a week, highlighting the fact that demand for Hawaii is strongest in winter.

Gemma Arterton is best known for playing Bond girl Strawberry Fields in the 2008 film Quantum of Solace, a role that earned her an Empire Award for Best Newcomer. Kate must navigate political loyalties, international threats, and personal sacrifice to expose them before her career and the investigation collapse.

Secret Service makes its official U.S. debut this month, following its U.K. premiere earlier this year. You want the tea, the gossip and the vicar behaving oddly. Midsomer is a county where the scenery is beautiful, the fete is on Saturday and the murder rate is roughly that of a small war, and absolutely nobody involved thinks this is strange.

You feel other people's grief as if it were handed to you personally. Broadchurch is less a whodunnit than a study of what a small town does to itself when it finds out that the answer was always going to be somebody everybody already knew.

You go through things rather than around them. John Luther is the best detective in London and a liability to everybody who works with him, and the show is completely honest that those two facts are the same fact.

You say the thing and you do not dress it up. Vera Stanhope turns up in a hat and a mac, is rude to almost everybody, and is the only person in the room prepared to ask the question that the family has been waiting years for somebody to ask.

Arterton next stars in The Portrait of a Lady, a six-episode TV adaptation premiering on MASTERPIECE on PBS in the U.S. and Canada, and on iPlayer and BBC One in the U.K. Based on Henry James's classic novel, the story follows Isabel Archer (portrayed by Nell Tiger Free), a vivacious young American who travels to Europe, eager to experience all life has to offer.

Charming and innately curious, Isabel turns down several marriage proposals. Southwest Airlines Auctioning Off Old Oakland Baggage Tugs For $750

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Southwest Airlines is auctioning off several electric baggage tugs previously used at Oakland San Francisco Bay Airport (OAK) in California, with starting bids of just $750 per vehicle. The used ground support equipment (GSE), dating back to 2008, has attracted online attention after its unusual auction listing was shared on Reddit. The sale gives aviation enthusiasts and potential buyers a chance to own a piece of airport equipment for far less than a conventional road vehicle.

The vehicles were originally designed to transport luggage and other equipment around airport aprons. While they may look like oversized utility carts, these specialized machines play an important role in airlines' daily operations, connecting aircraft with baggage-handling facilities and terminals. Their retirement from Oakland also highlights how airlines and airports periodically dispose of surplus equipment, creating opportunities for buyers outside the aviation industry.

According to PEOPLE, Southwest Airlines is offering several of its former baggage tugs through an online auction hosted on GovDeals. The vehicles were previously operated at Oakland San Francisco Bay Airport and are listed as 2008 Charlatte T137 electric baggage tugs. Each has a starting bid of $750, making them a relatively inexpensive proposition for anyone interested in acquiring unusual industrial machinery.

These vehicles are slightly larger than ordinary golf carts. Each tug measures approximately 10 feet, 5 inches (3.18 meters) long, 4 feet, 4 inches (1.32 meters) wide, and 4 feet (1.22 meters) tall. According to Charlatte America, the manufacturer, the T137 is a self-propelled, battery-powered electric tow tractor designed for airport ramp and industrial material-handling operations. Rather than carrying passengers, it is designed to move equipment and baggage trailers around airport facilities.

Importantly, the $750 figure represents the starting bid, so the final price can be higher. Buyers must also arrange and pay for collection of their successful purchases, and GovDeals requires winning bidders to schedule appointments to remove the equipment themselves. Consequently, prospective buyers must consider transportation, battery condition, maintenance requirements, and replacement-part availability.

Southwest also told Simple Flying that it periodically sells surplus equipment as part of its normal management of equipment and assets.

The auction quickly attracted attention beyond the aviation community after a listing was shared in the r/regularcarreviews Reddit community. The original poster joked about finding someone willing to buy one of the vehicles, prompting a lively discussion about possible alternative uses. Some commenters suggested using the tugs as neighborhood transport, while others imagined turning them into parade floats or even organizing an amateur racing competition.

One particularly practical suggestion was to use an airport tug to move agricultural equipment around a farm. Others pointed out that electric baggage tractors could potentially serve as unconventional utility vehicles for people with large properties. However, their airport-specific design means prospective owners would need to consider charging requirements, mechanical condition, operating limitations, and whether the vehicles could legally be used on public roads.

The Charlatte T137 illustrates the specialized nature of airport ground equipment. Baggage tugs are essential to ramp operations, moving multiple luggage trailers between aircraft and baggage handling areas. Their compact design allows them to maneuver around crowded aprons, while electric propulsion offers a clean alternative to conventional diesel-powered ground support vehicles. For airlines, maintaining a fleet of reliable tugs is essential to keeping aircraft turnaround operations running smoothly.

This is the story of Unclaimed Baggage, and how a $300 punt became a globe-spanning Alabama-based business.

The Oakland baggage tug sale is not an isolated example of airlines disposing of their surplus ground support equipment. In September 2024, Southwest Airlines organized a much larger auction through Starman Bros. Auctions, featuring approximately 119 pieces of equipment located at 31 airports across the United States. The inventory included 11 baggage tugs, six pushback tractors, nine ground power units, belt loaders, passenger stairs, heater carts, provisioning trucks, and six forklifts. The inventory also included several ordinary utility vehicles, golf carts, and workshop equipment.

Airports and airlines also periodically dispose of other items they no longer need, including old aircraft seats, cabin furniture, office equipment, and miscellaneous airport property. Even lost-and-found departments can generate unusual auction opportunities. A GovDeals listing illustrates this category, offering a collection of purses and bags classified as unclaimed lost-and-found luggage, sold without their contents and without guarantees about their condition or authenticity.

These sales can attract buyers interested in second-hand equipment, collectors, resellers, and people looking for unusual bargains. As explored by The Guardian, unclaimed luggage auctions have also become a popular curiosity, with buyers sometimes discovering valuable or unexpected belongings inside abandoned suitcases. Whether it is an electric baggage tug, an aircraft cabin seat, or an unclaimed suitcase, the aviation industry occasionally offers the public an unusual opportunity to acquire items that would otherwise remain within airport operations.

STAT+: FDA rejects lung cancer treatment ivonescimab from Summit Therapeutics

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Adam Feuerstein is a senior writer and biotech columnist, reporting on the crossroads of drug development, business, Wall Street, and biotechnology. He is also a co-host of the weekly biotech podcast The Readout Loud and author of the newsletter Adam’s Biotech Scorecard. You can reach Adam on Signal at stataf.54.

The Food and Drug Administration rejected a treatment for a type of lung cancer developed by the U.S.-based biotech Summit Therapeutics and the Chinese drugmaker Akeso, Summit said on Sunday.

In a letter sent to the companies, FDA regulators said the drug, called ivonescimab, could not be approved because it didn’t help patients live longer. While the drug was found to slow lung cancer worsening in patients, the absence of a survival benefit “does not support the positive benefit-risk profile in support of approval,” FDA reviewers concluded.

The FDA’s rejection of ivonescimab is a regulatory setback for Summit, which oversees development of the drug outside of China, although one that was not entirely unforeseen.

That gap has been filled with a theory that the U.S. military teleported the plane using three flying orbs. This narrative has gained traction and it’s now been featured (skeptically) in Rolling Stone and publicized by Candace Owens with over two and a half hours of airplay. The supposed video evidence appears to be stock images and special effects.

The aircraft stopped communicating with air traffic control, turned back across Malaysia, and continued flying for hours. Analysis of its satellite communications places the end of the flight in the southern Indian Ocean, likely after running out of fuel. Debris washed ashore in the western Indian Ocean, including a piece of the wing component found on Réunion in July 2015.

Investigators concluded that the turn back was likely made manually. I’ve always assumed pilot murder-suicide, but left room for the possibility of an extreme electrical failure. When the disaster happened, media attention was around the clock. I turned down numerous interview requests because I genuinely didn’t know what happened, had little useful to add, and did not wish to speculate on air.

His claim is that the U.S. secretly developed fusion-powered orbs that manipulate gravity and space-time. Three of them circled the Boeing 777, opened a portal, and transported it elsewhere. He now presents this as a demonstration of American power aimed at China.

But it reminds me of Millennium, the 1989 movie with Kris Kristofferson and Cheryl Ladd, where people from the future steal passengers from doomed airliners and leave substitute bodies behind to conceal what happened. Forbes adds flying orbs and gives the Pentagon credit for the technology. The full film is online.

Apparently the Pentagon’s solution to competition with China was to make a Malaysia Airlines flight disappear, then keep its spectacular demonstration secret from nearly everyone. Here’s Forbes’s repost of the footage, combining the supposed thermal drone recording with the supposed satellite view.

Forbes says he has government security clearance to establish credibility. But in his letter to Congress, he says his work has nothing to do with advanced technology and that the information he’s presenting is public.

The portal seems to use a stock special effect. The clouds appear to come from stock photos. Investigators matched the supposed satellite background to images on Textures.com taken over Japan in 2012. The cloud formations match. The animation seems to have compositing errors. West also describes trails that wobble relative to the aircraft and supposedly stereoscopic imagery made from distorted copies of the same view. High altitude, bitter cold, zero drivers: autonomous trucks take on Tibetan mine

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Finding qualified equipment operators is hard enough for most mining companies, and the extreme weather, punishing conditions, and oxygen-deprived air of the Tibetan mountains combine to make finding operators even tougher for the operators if this high-altitude copper mine – but SANY thinks they have a solution: fully self-driving haul trucks.

Chinese equipment brand SANY has deployed fully 60 of its 70-tonne (~80 ton) payload-rated SKT105ED battery electric haul trucks at the Zhibula Copper Mine in Tibet. Operated by Zijin Minig and Tibet Julong Copper, the mine functions at a dizzying 17,700 foot elevation in a region of the Gangdese Mountains known as the Kailas Range, and the conditions there would challenge the health and safety of even the toughest and fittest operators.

“High-altitude illness becomes increasingly relevant above 2,500 meters,” explains Eacon, in one section of a published paper. “Research involving workers in Andes mines operating between 3,800 and 4,500 meters found that 73% of workers assessed experienced periods of nocturnal oxygen saturation below 85% during their high-altitude shifts, indicating moderate-to-severe hypoxemia.”

That’s bad news for Zijin’s workers – but the good news here, of course, is that the company won’t be needing many heavy haul drivers going forward.

SANY has been working with Perth, Australia-based Eacon Mining Technology to develop an autonomous haulage solution that uses a full suite of both in-vehicle and ground-based sensors, adaptive vehicle controls, and machine learning that can handle the big bad trucks even here in the Kailas range.

“Autonomous haulage offers one way to address this challenge,” continues the Econ report. “Rather than requiring a driver for each truck, autonomous systems allow fleets to be managed through centralized supervision, with personnel overseeing operations and intervening when required. This reduces the need for people to spend entire shifts operating equipment at elevation, while changing how workforce resources are allocated across the mine … depending on the system configuration, some operations can manage autonomous fleets from remote Integrated Operations Centers located away from the mine site, (allowing) personnel to supervise haulage activities from lower-altitude locations, further reducing the need for workers to remain in extreme-altitude environments.”

The working partnership between the two companies combines SANY’s battery-powered truck platform with Eacon’s full technology stack to create dependable, driverless haul trucks with logic and behaviors that are ideally suited to China’s open-pit mining environments.

Eacon technology is currently operating more than 3,100 autonomous trucks cross more than 40 large-scale mines, including Zijin Mining’s Zijinshan Gold-Copper Mine and at the Norton Gold Fields project in Australia.

While specifications for this specific deployment weren’t disclosed, the SANY SKT105E trucks (the “D” is, presumably, the wide-body variant) the company has deployed elsewhere are powered by a dual-motor electric drivetrain, pairing two permanent-magnet synchronous motors with a dual three-speed automated manual transmission. The system delivers 570 kW (~765 hp) of continuous power and up to 820 kW (~1,100 hp) at peak output, with each motor producing some 2,430 Nm (~18,00 lb-ft) of peak torque.

Those motors get their electrons from an absolute whopper of a lithium iron phosphate (LFP) battery pack in capacities ranging from 423 to 642 kWh, depending on configuration. The big trucks also offer regenerative braking to recover energy on downhill runs, while their fast-charging system can reportedly charge the battery from 20% to 90% in under an hour.

The Design Fiend’s Guide to Tangier

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Moroccan city, a complex, culturally kaleidoscopic crossroads between Europe and Africa, is home to a savvy community of creatives whose stylish impact continues to grow. Word is out.

Above: The pool terrace at Villa Mabrouka, a 12-room hotel that was once the home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé, with gardens designed by Madison Cox.

Writing about Tangier in the 1950s, Truman Capote observed that “the days slide by less noticed than foam in a waterfall.” Decades later, this city on Morocco’s coast, across the Strait of Gibraltar from Spain, is significantly changed from Capote’s time, when it was an exotic retreat for café society’s demimonde, people such as Barbara Hutton and Malcolm Forbes. In recent years Tangier’s mosaic of villages—from the medina’s warren of whitewashed lanes to the Ville Moderne’s blocks of Art Deco towers to the hillside villas and gardens on Jebel Kebir—has been reinvigorated, in part by an enterprising mix of Tanjawis (the locals) and expats who continue to infuse the city with a fresh creative spirit.

Yet there is much that remains the same: the dreamy light that seduced artists from Eugène Delacroix to Henri Matisse to Mariano Fortuny, the overgrown gardens of casbah courtyards, and the cooling breezes coming off the confluence of Atlantic and Mediterranean waters. And there’s still plenty of the languorous lifestyle that captivated Capote.

“Tangier is all about settling in,” says Charlotte Claydon, whose whitewashed Moroccan Art Deco house has been in her family for more than a century. Known as Dar Sinclair, the residence sits in a grove of papyrus and lilies between thick palms and weeping willows. It’s one of numerous historic villas and townhouses renovated by international designers and other creatives who live here that can be rented. There’s arguably no better way to ease into the rhythms of daily life in Tangier. Pin Affleck, who arranges travel and accommodations through the Tangier Bureau, says that “going into a private house, you get the sense of being inside Tangier.”

When Claydon and her sister Jacquetta Wheeler began renting out Dar Sinclair, they received messages from guests worried about filling up a week. “We spent hours coming up with lists of activities, knowing full well they might only get to one or two,” Claydon says, noting that most guests make their own discoveries and end up on unplanned diversions.

Among the sisters’ can’t-miss activities: tea at Café de Paris, Sunday service at historic St. Andrew’s Church, and a camel ride, especially with kids, at Achakar Beach. A typical morning in Tangier might begin with breakfast under a banyan tree at El Morocco Club. Around 11 a.m. the markets buzz to life, including the legendary fish market in the Grand Socco, the Fondouk Chejra (weavers’ market), the antiques mall on Rue de Hollande, and the Casabarata flea market, where a sharp eye is required to spot the gems amid the seedy furniture and cheap fabrics.

Lunch in town at Alma Kitchen or El Dorado is a must for crispy falafel and plates of lamb chops. Later, as the sun sinks into the sea, it’s hard to beat the views from the roof of Villa Mabrouka, the onetime home of Yves Saint Laurent and Pierre Bergé that Jasper Conran transformed into hotel with gardens by Tangier resident Madison Cox. After a martini, the hotel’s L’Orangerie restaurant is a smart spot for a dinner of thistle tagine and grilled sole; down the street, the atmospheric Hamadi serves standout harissa soup and sizzling skewers.

Claydon and Wheeler, for their part, also advise guests to reserve time for relaxing and being transported at home, one of the great pleasures of staying in a design-centric private residence. Other places available to rent include the red-and-white-striped riad owned by decorator Gavin Houghton and photographer Boz Gagovski (who took most of the photos in this story), as well as antiques dealer Gordon Watson’s large ochre house. Both weave together Moorish and European details, with objects from Tangier and around the world that reflect the city’s cosmopolitan history. As Hamish Bowles noted in his foreword to the book Inside Tangier, “The magic of this place was that everyone who had chosen to make a home here brought their own fantasy with them.”

That book was produced by my husband, designer Nicolò Castellini Baldissera, and our home here, a 1929 townhouse named Casa Tosca, is filled with a worldly mix of objects and furnishings that we designed. Outside, our terraced garden was overseen by Umberto Pasti, whose own Tangier residence is a collection of 1930s bungalows set within meandering gardens of datura trees, rambling roses, and alocasia. For us, so much of the pleasure of this place is being at home and having people over. Creating the setting with local craftspeople and market finds becomes a pastime in itself.

It’s the same for Marco Scarani and Jamie Creel, whose house, Dar Zero, has had an illustrious lineage of owners who have contributed to its rich layers. The home is in the casbah, with a bushy fig tree and bougainvillea spilling from its courtyard over the front wall. Inside, iron klismos chairs from when designers Yves Vidal and Charles Sevigny lived there sit on well-worn bejmat and zellige tiles. There are also pieces of their own creation, such as brass bird-foot candlesticks they make in Tangier and sell in their New York shop, Creel & Gow. “There is a lot of transmission from one generation to another,” Scarani says. “Friends here inherit objects and even homes. We live in houses that have the spirits of the people who came before.”

For designers Frank de Biasi and Gene Meyer, it was the relationship with artisans that prompted them a few years ago to open Habibi Burton, their boutique where they offer pillows, painted tables, and a rotation of decorative objects, all sourced locally. “Making things for the shop gives me more freedom than making things for clients,” says de Biasi. “Because we aren’t designing to a deadline, I can have four artisans contribute to a single piece.” He also recommends Las Chicas for fashion, Kasbah Collective for regional crafts, and the bookshop Les Insolites.

Down the street, artist and curator Hicham Bouzid oversees Kiosk, a cultural salon and shop that hosts talks and exhibitions bringing together creatives from Africa, the Arab world, and Europe. It has a selection of books and artist prints, as well as a restaurant and live music. Bouzid is one of the native Tanjawis who are animating the city’s cultural life. Another is Yto Barrada, perhaps Morocco’s best-known contemporary artist, who founded the popular Cinéma Rif, where screenings of contemporary and classic films share the program with talks and other cultural events.

Across town Clos Nadir, the house shared by designer Jean-Louis Deniot and William Holloway, exists in a different world, perched among pines and looking out across the strait. Focusing on spaces for entertaining, Deniot placed a 30-foot dining table on a roof, while his living room is outfitted with a large fireplace, plush carpets, and a welcoming mix of furniture, mostly made locally. “Tangier is a land of artistic opportunity, a place for aesthetes, where stylistic adventures, contemplation, and the freedom to create are central to daily life,” says the ELLE Decor A-List designer, who is opening an office here.

Tangier’s close-knit community of creatives comes alive during the summer, which is when Veere Greeney, another ELLE Decor A-List designer, hosts an annual theatrical performance in the gardens of his mountaintop villa, Gazebo. Producer Rob Ashford assembles actors from Broadway and the West End (last summer it was Dames at Sea, featuring Darren Criss and Julianne Hough), and the shows are open to the public, though tickets are limited. It’s a full sensory experience colored by the scent of jasmine and gardenias, one that captures the eccentric artistic allure of Tangier.

The energy and interest in the city continue to grow. Deniot is creating a hotel with theater producer Jordan Roth, the Four Seasons recently announced a new property on Malabata Bay, and King Mohammed VI is behind a luxurious renovation of the historic Hotel Continental.

And while there is excitement around these new projects, what appeals most about Tangier is something older. Over lunch on Grenney’s terrace, looking out at Spain, we discussed the city’s past lives. “What makes Tangier unique is its varied history and strategic position,” he observed. “The strait made it a city of diplomacy, openness, and inclusiveness. That is its bedrock.”

This story originally appeared in the November 2026 issue of ELLE Decor.

Liverpool eye early deal for €70m-rated target after summer transfer plans changed

Liverpool are reportedly considering an early move for Monaco midfielder Lamine Camara as they look to strengthen their options in the middle of the park ahead of the January transfer window.

According to James Pearce, the Reds could accelerate their pursuit of the Senegal international, with developments suggesting a move to Anfield may be closer than initially thought.

The 22-year-old has attracted considerable attention with his performances in France, and Liverpool have been monitoring his situation for some time. The Merseyside outfit views him as an attractive option to improve their midfield, particularly amid concerns surrounding injuries to key players.

In an interview with The Redmen TV, journalist James Pearce suggested Liverpool could bring forward their plans to sign Camara rather than waiting until next summer. He also pointed to a situation similar to Luis Diaz’s arrival in January 2022, when Liverpool moved quickly to secure the Colombian winger.

“It wouldn’t surprise me if something similar happened [to the Diaz signing] in terms of someone who was on the list for the summer.

“I think what happens with Lamine Camara at Monaco will be interesting, because we know that had Liverpool decided to buy a senior midfielder last summer, that he probably would have been the one.

“Say if [Alexis] Mac Allister had turned around and said he wanted to go, I think that would’ve been the most likely outcome, that Mac Allister went and Camara came in.

“But, of course, there’s so many moving parts in terms of what does the player want, what do the club he’s at at the minute want in terms of are they determined to keep hold of him until the summer.”

Camara’s potential arrival would provide Liverpool with another technically gifted midfielder capable of adding energy, defensive intensity and creativity to their engine room.

The Senegalese international was previously close to joining Chelsea during the summer transfer window, but the proposed move collapsed at the final stage. Liverpool have since emerged as serious contenders for his signature.

Monaco are reportedly prepared to sanction his departure, although they are expected to demand around €70 million (£58m) for the midfielder. Liverpool could initially test their resolve with an offer worth approximately £42m.

Chelsea have also been monitoring Bournemouth midfielder Alex Scott, and their interest could give Liverpool a clearer path to securing Camara.

It remains to be seen whether the Reds can reach an agreement with Monaco, but an early January move could prove an important piece of business for the Premier League giants.

Holiday Spending Cap – Set It at What You Can Pay Off in January

📅 Nov 15, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Holiday shopping on a credit card stays interest-free only if you can pay the whole balance when January’s statement comes due. So before you shop, add your holiday savings to what your next few paychecks leave after regular bills, use that total as your spending cap, and track every purchase against it.

In ICSC’s 2026 Holiday Intentions Survey, 23% of the adults surveyed said they expect to still be paying for this season’s purchases in 2027.

Most credit cards charge no interest on purchases when you pay the full statement balance, the total your bill shows, by the due date each month. Whatever part of the holiday bill you leave unpaid in January becomes debt at the card’s interest rate.

In August, bank credit card accounts that were charged interest paid an average rate of 22.36%, according to the Federal Reserve. Say $1,000 is still on the card after January’s payment, you pay $100 a month from then on, and you charge nothing new. At that rate, clearing it takes 12 payments and about $117 in interest, and the last payment is due in January 2028, after next year’s holidays.

So set the cap before Black Friday, Nov. 27, from money that will be in your checking account by the January due date. Start with what your holiday savings hold now, and add whatever each paycheck before then leaves after rent, utilities, and other regular bills. That total is the cap, not what you’d like to spend or your card’s credit limit.

Count every holiday cost against it, not just gifts, since food, travel, decorations, and year-end tips for a sitter or house cleaner come out of the same money. Then put every holiday purchase on one card, keep everyday spending off it, and check its balance once a week. When the total gets close to the cap, trim the gift list rather than raise the cap.

Two things mean the cap was set too high: a balance you won’t be able to clear in January, or a gift you’d need to split into buy now, pay later installments to afford. Both mean the season costs more than the money you’ll have by January, so lower the cap or drop the purchase.

Write the cap at the top of your gift list this week, beside the date January’s payment is due.
